Back the camp

Program proposal · Summer 2027 · Ages 10–14

AI can make almost anything. It can't check anything. We teach kids the second half.

Ground Truth Summer Camp is a two-week day camp where kids become producers for AI projects, the humans who collect real data, verify claims, build the physical thing, exercise judgment, and put their name on the result. Every day, campers do the exact work AI is demonstrably, almost magically bad at.

Format10 one-hour lessons
Cohort12 campers
Ratio1 instructor : 12 kids
Hours60 minutes a day
See the 10-day curriculum Get the full proposal

01 · Mission

Raise producers, not prompters.

The scarcest skill of the next decade isn't prompting an AI. It's knowing, with evidence, when the AI is wrong. Kids today grow up as native consumers of generated content. Nobody is teaching them to be the verifier, the builder, the person accountable for the output. That's this camp's entire reason to exist.

The thesis Generative AI can produce a plausible anything, a photo, a fact, a 3D model, a sympathetic sentence. What it cannot do is go outside, touch the object, interview the person, measure the claim, notice that it's wrong, or take responsibility when it is. Those aren't bugs to fix; they're the permanent human share of the work. We train kids for that share, on purpose, with real tools, for real stakes.

What a producer does

On a real AI project, the producer sets the goal, feeds the model real data, runs the quality gates, fixes what the machine gets wrong, and signs their name to the final product. It's the highest-leverage, least-automable role on the team. Our campers practice every piece of that job at kid scale: they direct AI tools constantly, but the deliverables only ship if they collected the data, checked the facts, tested the object, and can defend the result.

Why ages 10–14

This is the age when kids form their default relationship with technology. They're old enough to use real tools, cameras, calipers, 3D printers, soldering irons (supervised), interview recorders, and young enough that "the computer said so" hasn't yet hardened into an assumption. We catch them right before it does.

02 · Why now

The generation being raised on AI output has no training in AI's failure modes.

By 2026, school-age kids routinely meet generated text, images, and voices before they meet the verified kind. Meanwhile, every working professional knows the new bottleneck: generation is cheap, ground truth is expensive. The adults who thrive are the ones who can produce and verify. The kids who learn it at eleven will run circles around the ones who learn it at twenty-five.

What AI is magically bad atWhat that means in practiceWhere campers do it
Knowing the real worldIt can't take a photo, feel a wobble, or smell a burnt motor. Its "facts" about today are stale or invented.Days 2–4 · data collection & sensors
Checking its own workIt states falsehoods with total confidence, numbers, quotes, citations, and cannot notice.Days 1, 3, 8 · verification & QC
Making physical thingsIt can describe a bracket; it cannot hold one. Generated designs fail on real tolerances and real loads.Days 5, 7, 9 · fabrication & robotics
Judging tasteIt optimizes for average, not for right. It cannot tell "correct" from "good."Days 1, 8, 10 · taste panels
Understanding peopleIt simulates empathy from text. It can't sit across from a human and earn a real answer.Day 6 · interviews
Being accountableNo AI can take responsibility for a failure. Someone human always has to.Every day; formally Day 9

03 · Curriculum

Ten days, two arcs: collect the truth, then produce with it.

Week 1 is Ground Truth, going into the field and the workshop to gather and check what AI can't. Week 2 is The Producer's Chair, directing AI tools on real projects and owning every result. Every day names the weakness it attacks, runs a 60-minute instructor-led lesson with a minute-by-minute run-of-show, and continues into a 45-minute self-directed project block with a printed handout. AI tools are used daily, always as the assistant, never the author.

Week 1 · Ground Truth

Days 1-5 · the world AI can't touch

Each day is one 60-minute instructor-led lesson with a minute-by-minute run-of-show, followed by a 45-minute self-directed project block campers complete from a printed handout, in the same room. The block needs no adult instruction: the handout, the answer key, and the wall rubric do the teaching, and the instructor stays in the room, silent, supervising. Every day also lists its exact materials and a prep checklist completable by one person in 30 minutes.

Day 1 · Monday

The Blind Taste Test

0:00-0:10Open. Welcome, the three-rule room contract set in three minutes (the safety plan's behavior procedure), then the evacuation walk: the whole group walks the venue's evacuation route to the assembly point, the instructor counts all twelve aloud, then ends the drill aloud and leads the group back inside together, the safety plan's emergency rehearsal done before any tool or material is touched. The drill's walk back is the announced rehearsal; the no-re-entry rule in the safety plan governs real alarms only. The "AI Fail of the Day" follows as a two-minute teaser, one real, recent blunder, because the full teardown plays on the room screen in the project block. Buddy pairs read off the buddy-pair board (six first-name pairs, alphabetical roster order, set by the instructor, never chosen by campers), buddy sits beside buddy in the U; teams of four assigned; seats set for the session.
0:10-0:40The Gauntlet. Each team gets a tray of 20 unlabeled artifacts (photos, essays, poems, product designs; half AI-made, half human). Campers vote human-or-AI on answer cards. The instructor circulates and asks the dangerous question: "What made you sure?"
0:40-0:55Reveal + rubric build. The instructor reads the answers team by team from the Gauntlet answer key in the instructor folder. The shock is the point: confident guesses land near 50%. The group writes the camp's shared rubric, "How do we know something is true?", on the poster that hangs on the lab wall all session.
0:55-1:00Handoff. Each camper gets a laminated Producer's Checklist and the project-block handout, and finds their name-labeled portfolio folder on the shelf. Every project block ends with filing that day's artifact. The instructor demos the scan-tab once at the flatbed scanner, one sticky-note tab on a journal page, so the gallery paper path is seen, not just printed, before the session's first pickup. Crew resets the trays, packs the 3 reset trays back into the Day 1 box at the instructor table, and returns the 4 markers to the equipment shelf; the HDMI cable and the USB-C-to-HDMI adapter unplug from the room screen and coil back onto the equipment shelf.
If it runs longIf the vote runs past 0:40, the instructor cuts the team-by-team reveal to a single three-minute score readout and the rubric poster is finished in the last 10 minutes of the project block. The trays are reset by crew during the handoff and packed back into the Day 1 box at the instructor table, never by stealing teaching time.
Materials, 12 campers, pre-kitted
  • 3 artifact trays x 20 pre-printed, numbered cards (60 cards total), one per team table
  • 12 answer cards (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6) + 12 laminated producer checklists (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1 and laminated in Hour 2, see Phase 6)
  • 12 camp journals (composition notebooks, one per camper, the session notebook; goes home daily)
  • 12 portfolio folders (expanding file folders, one per camper; live in the Day 1 box; named in prep, they live on the room's portfolio shelf all session, never in backpacks)
  • 3 AI Fail binders, one per team table (1-inch 3-ring binders, reusable shop stock, bought once; the 12 printed real-world blunders per binder (the spring's selected 12, see Phase 6) slide in at Session Zero Hour 2, refreshed each year)
  • Truth-rubric poster (copy-shop print; the class writes the shared rubric on it in the lesson, so it is one per session, not reusable shop stock; packed in the Day 1 box at Session Zero, pulled in prep) + crew-rotation board (one page, printed at Session Zero Hour 1 and laminated in Hour 2; packed in the Day 1 box, hung beside the poster in prep; one per session, names written from the session roster) + buddy-pair board (one page, printed at Session Zero Hour 1 and laminated in Hour 2; packed in the Day 1 box, hung beside the crew board in prep; one per session, first names only, written from the session roster) + 4 markers (reusable shop stock; staged on the equipment shelf at Session Zero, pulled from the shelf in prep)
  • 1 pre-loaded "AI Fail of the Day" slide + the one-take teardown video file (both on the instructor laptop; the teardown plays on the room screen in the Day 1 project block)
  • 1 HDMI cable (6 ft) + 1 USB-C-to-HDMI adapter (reusable shop stock; staged and signal-tested against the venue room screen at Session Zero Hour 4; pulled from the equipment shelf in prep for the room-screen connection; unplugged and returned to the shelf at the handoff)
  • 12 project-block handouts, "Failure Autopsy" (live in the Day 1 box; issued at the handoff)
  • 12 everyday ballpoint pens (one per camper, the camp pen; reusable shop stock, about $8 for twelve, bought once inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line; live in the Day 1 box; staged at each seat with the camp journals in prep; each camper keeps their pen with their journal all session, so no collection step)
Prep checklist, one person, about 30 min
  • Open the Day 1 box; lay out 3 trays, one per team table, and one AI Fail binder per team table
  • Place 12 answer cards, checklists, a camp journal, and one camp pen at each seat
  • Name-label the 12 portfolio folders; stand them on the labeled portfolio shelf on the side wall (about 4 min, existing room shelving)
  • Pull the truth-rubric poster from the Day 1 box and hang it on the lab wall, then hang the laminated crew-rotation board beside it (it lives beside the poster all session; the 9:30 open's crew assignments read off it, so the instructor never chooses names on the fly); then hang the laminated buddy-pair board beside the crew board (first names only, per the Day 9 rule; the 9:15 drop-off's late-arrival seat, every field walk, the dose gate, and the incident form all name a buddy pair, and this board is the only place the pairs are assigned); pull the 4 markers off the equipment shelf; test the markers
  • Pull the HDMI cable and the USB-C-to-HDMI adapter off the equipment shelf; plug the instructor laptop into the room screen; open the pre-loaded fail-of-the-day slide and the teardown video file; confirm the projector
The weakness attackedAI has no idea when it's wrong. Neither, yet, do we. Today fixes the second part.
Deliverable · Personal producer's checklist + team truth-rubric poster on the lab wall
Project block (self-directed, 45 min) · Handout: "Failure Autopsy" (full text in §03b below). Campers watch the pre-recorded teardown of the two real AI disasters specified in the handout, add three more from the printed AI Fail binder to their journals, and finish the checklist's Day 1 items. Answer key lives in the instructor folder; the rubric poster answers the hard questions.
Day 2 · Tuesday

Data Safari

0:00-0:10Open + kit issue. Cameras, lav mics, clipboards, mission cards. Mission brief: "document every plant species on the block." Rules: the cohort moves as one group, inside the chalk boundary, cameras always on straps. Juniors GPS-tag each photo; seniors add a metadata schema (species, angle, light, confidence).
0:10-0:40Data safari. One block, single group, instructor at the front. Each camper shoots at least 25 photos and logs angles, lighting, and edge cases on the tally sheet. The instructor calls out the boring shots models never see: undersides, damaged leaves, half-hidden stems.
0:40-0:55Label party. Back inside. The first five minutes (10:10-10:15) are the upload beat: the three crew members each pull one team's SD card from its camera, plug it into one of the three USB SD card readers on the three reader laptops by the grading station, and drag the card's photos into the Day 2 shared album (created at Session Zero on the camp photos account, the album link bookmarked on all 8 cart laptops). Three cards in parallel, about 100 photos each, done by 10:15 on the venue Wi-Fi the Hour 3 test timed; once a drag starts, its crew member rejoins their team. Teams open the album on their cart laptops and start tagging whatever has landed; the cards stay in the readers until the block ends, so a failed upload is re-dragged, not debugged. Then the party proper: each team runs an image model on 10 of their own plants on one of the cart laptops (the model tab loads on all 8 at Session Zero; the grading-station machine is the instructor's alone for the contact-sheet assembly below), and grades it against their labels. Every miss goes on the Wall of Almost. Each camper flags their own 20 best photos in the shared album. While the teams work the album, the instructor works the grading-station laptop: the 12 contact-sheet pages are assembled there, pulling each camper's 20 live flags into the pre-built template (20 numbered thumbnails each with label lines), and the job queues to the venue printer by 10:24, so the handoff is only the print run, not a surprise fifteen minutes of layout.
0:55-1:00Handoff. "The Labeler" handout issued. The pre-assembled 12-page contact-sheet job runs on the venue printer during the last five minutes of the lesson (10:25-10:30): 12 pages, 20 numbered thumbnails each with label lines, about 3 minutes, so the project block runs on paper with no laptop. Crew collects the cameras for charging on the labeled charging tray; the crew strips the 12 used tally sheets off the 12 field clipboards and files each into its camper's portfolio folder on the portfolio shelf, the raw safari field log filed behind the contact-sheet the project block produces, and stacks the 12 emptied field clipboards back into the Day 2 box at the instructor table; the lav mics, calipers, the 3 kit clipboards, and chalk are packed back into the 3 field kits, the recorders riding inside, and the kits return to the equipment shelf until Day 6, so the next session's Day 2 prep pulls the clipboards out of the Day 2 box and loads fresh tally sheets onto the emptied boards. Crew collects the 12 laminated mission cards from the team tables back into the Day 2 box at the instructor table, so the next session's Day 2 prep finds the cards where the checklist says they are, not loose on the tables. The grading-station laptop goes back on the laptop cart, plugged in to charge.
If it runs longIf the safari runs past 0:40, the label party grades 10 photos per team instead of the full album. The remaining tagging is exactly what the project block is for. If one card's upload is still copying at 10:15, that team's crew member stays on the reader laptop until the drag lands; the team tags from the two cards already in the album, and the instructor queues that team's contact-sheet page last in the print job so its 20 flags have until 10:28 to land.
Materials, 12 campers, pre-kitted
  • 3 field kits (camera, lav mic, recorder, calipers, clipboard, chalk), one per team of 4; the cameras pair in from the labeled charging tray in prep, the recorders ride inside the sealed kits from Session Zero
  • 12 tally sheets (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6) + 12 mission cards (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1 and laminated in Hour 2, see Phase 6)
  • 12 field clipboards, one per camper (reusable shop stock, about $20 for twelve; bought once inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line; staged in the Day 2 box at Session Zero and loaded with the tally sheets in prep; the field kits' own 3 clipboards stay in the kits and are never borrowed)
  • Wall of Almost poster board (a copy-shop print, picked up Saturday morning with the truth-rubric poster) + grading sheet ("AI vs. you") (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6; live in the Day 2 box); pulled in prep and hung beside the grading station
  • 12 printed photo contact-sheets (20 numbered thumbnails each, queued from the grading-station laptop during the label party, printed during the last five minutes of the lesson (10:25-10:30); the contact-sheet is the label sheet)
  • 12 red pens (one per camper, for the QC swap; reusable shop stock, about $8 for twelve, bought once inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line; live in the Day 2 box)
  • 1 laptop at the grading station (the labeled grading-station cart laptop), image-model tab open, content filter on, contact-sheet template open (the 20-thumbnail template lives on this machine from the Session Zero load; it returns to the cart at the handoff)
  • 3 USB SD card readers + 3 of the cart laptops as reader laptops, staged by the grading station (the readers are reusable shop stock, about $12 for three, staged on the equipment shelf at Session Zero; pulled from the shelf and plugged into the reader laptops in prep; the Day 2 shared album opens in the browser on the grading-station laptop so the instructor sees all three uploads land)
  • 12 project-block handouts, "The Labeler" (live in the Day 2 box; issued at the handoff)
Prep checklist, one person, about 25 min
  • Pull the 3 field kits off the equipment shelf (the recorders ride inside, charged and paired at Session Zero, untouched)
  • Pull the 3 cameras off the labeled charging tray; confirm the charge lights; wipe memory cards; pair one camera with each staged field kit before issue
  • Pull the 12 field clipboards out of the Day 2 box and load the tally sheets onto them; stack mission cards
  • Pull the labeled grading-station laptop off the laptop cart and set the grading station; test the model tab and filter; open the pre-built 20-thumbnail contact-sheet template (the instructor assembles the 12 pages here during the 0:40-0:55 label party, so the template is open before the campers come back inside)
  • Pull three cart laptops off the laptop cart and stage them by the grading station as the reader laptops; pull the 3 USB SD card readers off the equipment shelf and plug one into each reader laptop; sign the grading-station laptop into the camp photos account, open the Day 2 shared album in the browser, and leave it open; confirm the album-link bookmark is on all 8 cart laptops (the three crew members run the upload beat on these readers at 10:10)
  • Pull the Wall of Almost poster board, the grading sheet ("AI vs. you"), and the 12 red pens from the Day 2 box; hang the poster board beside the grading station, lay the grading sheet there, and set the red pens out at the team tables for the QC swap in the project block
  • Confirm the venue printer is online and loaded with paper; the 20-photo contact-sheets print during the last five minutes of the lesson (10:25-10:30)
  • Walk the block once and lay the chalk boundary (on a weather-pivot morning, skip the walk: the safari route is the makerspace interior and hallway, per the safety plan, and the chalk stays in the kit)
The weakness attackedAI only knows the world from data humans collected. Fresh, local, weird reality is invisible to it.
Deliverable · A cleaned, labeled, human-verified dataset (first entry in each camper's portfolio)
Project block (self-directed, 45 min) · Handout: "The Labeler" (full text in §03b below). Finish labeling your 20 flagged photos on the contact-sheet, QC a partner's sheet, and write one paragraph, "what the model missed and why." Answer key: the common misses are backlit leaves and damaged plants; the poster has the checklist. At block end, crew collects the 12 red pens from the team tables back into the Day 2 box, pulls the 3 SD cards from the readers back into their cameras on the labeled charging tray, unplugs the 3 USB SD card readers from the reader laptops and returns them to the equipment shelf (their Session Zero staging home; Friday's print station needs one of them), and returns the 3 reader laptops to the laptop cart, plugged in to charge.
Day 3 · Wednesday

The Verification Bureau

0:00-0:10Claim intake. Each team gets a stack of 5 AI-generated "facts" about the town (population, founding year, what's at Main and 3rd). Campers rank their confidence on the claim sheet and write one line: "how I'd check it."
0:10-0:40Verification sprint. Single-group walk to two scripted stops (a shop owner briefed the week before, a public plaque). The shop-owner stop is a walk-up at the storefront, not a visit inside: per the safety plan's no-entering-buildings rule, the interview happens outside and the group never enters the shop. All three teams work in parallel at each stop: eight minutes per stop, two stops, sixteen minutes of field time. Measuring tape out, questions asked, photos taken; every team stays inside the group and the instructor runs the clock. The rest of the thirty minutes is about ten minutes of walking between stops plus slack. The clock time is eight minutes per stop, never eight minutes per team.
0:40-0:55Verdicts. Back inside. Each camper fills one verdict card, verified, busted, or unverifiable, with evidence attached (photo, measurement, primary source); a team's four cards spread over four different claims of the team's five, the fifth claim riding the project block. The instructor demos citing a primary source in one minute; each team reads one verdict aloud. The phrase of the day: "we couldn't confirm it."
0:55-1:00Handoff. "The Verdict Report" handout issued; remaining claims are finished on it, in the project block or at home with a parent. Crew collects the 12 filled verdict cards off the team tables and files each into its camper's portfolio folder on the portfolio shelf, the lesson's verdict card filed in front of the verdict-report template the project block files at its 0:40-0:45 sign step. Crew collects the 3 cameras back to the labeled charging tray; the 3 measuring tapes go back in the Day 3 box; the 3 clipboards go back into the 3 field kits on the equipment shelf.
If it runs longIf the walk runs past 0:40, the instructor demos one verdict aloud and each team finishes two cards in the room. The remaining claims move to the project block; the answer key is the pre-verification sheet.
Materials, 12 campers, pre-kitted
  • 3 claim stacks x 5 cards (all claims pre-checked by the instructor against primary sources the week before the session; sealed in the Day 3 box at Session Zero, never re-researched on a teaching morning)
  • 12 verdict cards (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6) + 12 verdict-report templates (one per camper; laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6) + 3 measuring tapes + 3 field clipboards (the verdict cards, the verdict-report templates, and the 3 measuring tapes live in the Day 3 box; the 3 field clipboards ride in the 3 field kits, packed back in at the Day 2 handoff)
  • 3 field-kit cameras, one per team (pulled from the labeled charging tray in prep, returned to it at handoff); shop-owner briefing sheet (instructor folder)
  • 12 project-block handouts, "The Verdict Report" (live in the Day 3 box; issued at the handoff)
Prep checklist, one person, about 30 min
  • Pull the sealed pre-verified claim stack from the Day 3 box; count 15 cards
  • Reconfirm the shop owner's storefront visit window by text (the walk-up happens outside; briefed the week before; the briefing sheet carries the weather-pivot line, so on a pivot morning this text becomes the 9:40 speakerphone call)
  • Set the two-stop walking route; print the route card, which carries a printed 911 location line (route street, nearest cross street, mapped hard shelter) set when the route was mapped at the T-2 site visit, plus the T-2-marked dose gate beside the 911 line (on a weather-pivot morning, skip the walk: run the two indoor verifications from the safety plan, the speakerphone call and the emailed plaque photo)
  • Pull the 3 field-kit cameras off the labeled charging tray; confirm the charge lights; pull the 3 measuring tapes out of the Day 3 box and test them; confirm the 3 clipboards are still riding in the 3 field kits
The weakness attackedAI states invented specifics with total confidence and cannot check a single one.
Deliverable · Evidence-backed verdicts on all 15 AI claims, at least 3 field-measured: the lesson's 12 verdict cards plus every camper's two template verdicts cover the full claim set, and the three signed team reports are each team's two-claim official record
Project block (self-directed, 45 min) · Handout: "The Verdict Report" (full text in §03b below). Write the verdict report on the template: claim, verdict, evidence, source line. Answer key is the instructor's pre-verification sheet; it stays in the instructor folder.
Day 4 · Thursday

Sensors and Signal

0:00-0:10Demo. The instructor holds up the finished station, names every part, and sets the safety gate: the solder station runs only while the instructor stands at it. No one touches the iron during the demo; the rotation order for the build block goes up on the wall now, written with one of the 4 markers pulled from the equipment shelf in prep, and everyone else wires sensors or writes code.
0:10-0:40Build. Pairs rotate through the single heated station (5 minutes each, timed, queue posted on the wall) while the rest assemble sensor boards and write the logging program on laptops. The instructor runs the solder station personally, the full 30 minutes, with ventilation and PPE (personal protective equipment). Juniors follow the wiring-diagram card; seniors calibrate and add a second sensor channel.
0:40-0:55Deploy + compare. The whole group places stations around the venue and yard, inside the instructor's line of sight the entire time (on a weather-pivot morning, the stations deploy around the room instead; the overnight row is already indoors, so the log, the harvest, and the predict-check arc run unchanged). First readings go on the log sheet. Compare them against the printed AI "forecast" sheet on your team table, and discuss: why is fresh measured data worth more than a fluent prediction?
0:55-1:00Handoff. Overnight logging charts issued. Friday's opening is harvest-and-graph, ten minutes. Crew brings the 12 sensor stations back inside and parks them on the numbered spots of the room's overnight row on the windowsill, where they keep logging indoor air overnight; each pair presses button B on its station as it parks it, resetting the min/max trackers so Friday's reading is the overnight window only. The stations sleep in the room, never outside, never in a camper's backpack. The crew stows the extractor and the 6 safety glasses back in the Day 4 box under the instructor's eye; the Day 4 burn kit rides back into the Day 4 box beside the station, so the next session's Day 4 prep pulls it from the box where the checklist says it is, not from somewhere beside the station; then the instructor locks the cooled station, cord wrapped, in the box with them before the project block starts. Crew collects the 12 wiring-diagram cards from the stations back into the Day 4 box at the instructor table, so the next session's Day 4 prep finds the cards where the checklist says they are, not scattered across the stations. Crew returns the 4 markers to the equipment shelf.
If it runs longThe queue is the schedule: 6 pairs at 5 minutes each fill the 30 minutes exactly. If a pair runs over, the instructor finishes their joints so the queue holds; nobody misses a slot, and no soldering happens outside this window. If a station is dead at 0:40, that pair joins the nearest working pair for the deploy and compare.
Materials, 12 campers, pre-kitted
  • 12 micro:bit sensor kits (temp, light, sound; USB rechargeable); 2 spare USB cables on the equipment shelf with the labeled charging tray
  • 1 pre-built reference sensor station (a thirteenth station, not one of the twelve camper kits; the finished station the 0:00-0:10 demo holds up to name every part; wired at Session Zero Hour 3 to match the week-before wiring-diagram card, then button-A tested; reusable shop stock, lives in the Day 4 box; about $71, absorbed in the $1,500 shop-tools startup line, same treatment as the Day 4 graphing rulers)
  • 1 soldering station (instructor-run only) + fume extractor + 6 safety glasses (live in the Day 4 box; staged and tested at Session Zero Hour 4; locked in the box between uses; bought once as reusable shop stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line, about $90 for the set); 1 solder-wire spool (60/40 rosin core, one fresh spool per session, live in the Day 4 box); 1 spare extractor filter (live in the Day 4 box; a running filter that reads loaded swaps out at the Thursday prep check, about two minutes inside the 30-minute checklist, and the Session Zero Hour 4 stage replaces any spare a prior session consumed, off the Day 4 consumables row's extractor filter share)
  • 1 Day 4 burn kit (burn gel packets, burn dressings, instant cold packs; bought once as reusable shop stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line, about $25; lives in the Day 4 box beside the soldering station; stocked and staged at Session Zero Hour 4; pulled beside the station in the Day 4 prep, returned to the box at the handoff)
  • 12 wiring-diagram cards (live in the Day 4 box; printed at Session Zero Hour 1 from the week-before build, see Phase 6); logging program pre-loaded on laptops + the logging-program USB stick (live in the Day 4 box; bought once as reusable shop stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line) as the fallback
  • 3 printed AI forecast sheets, one per team table (this spring's AI weather forecast, printed from the laptop); 12 overnight log charts (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6; live in the Day 4 box)
  • 12 deploy log sheets + 12 graph charts (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6; live in the Day 4 box; the morning deploy's raw readings go on the log sheet, the hand-graph goes on the chart, which pins to the wall at the block's end)
  • 12 clear 12-inch plastic rulers (one per camper; reusable shop stock, live in the Day 4 box; these are the graphing rulers, not the cutting station's metal ruler)
  • 4 markers (reusable shop stock; staged on the equipment shelf at Session Zero; pulled from the shelf in prep for the rotation order; returned to the shelf at the handoff)
  • 12 project-block handouts, "The Sensor Read" (live in the Day 4 box; issued at the handoff)
Prep checklist, one person, about 25 min
  • Pull the 12 micro:bits off the labeled charging tray (their named home since Session Zero); confirm the charge lights; lay out the kits, the 12 wiring-diagram cards, the 12 rulers, the 12 deploy log sheets, the 12 graph charts, and the overnight log charts
  • Pull the pre-built reference sensor station out of the Day 4 box and stage it at the instructor table for the 0:00-0:10 demo; it goes back in the Day 4 box at the handoff, so the next session's Day 4 prep finds it where the checklist says it is, not loose on the table
  • Pull the locked soldering station out of the Day 4 box; set it up, test the iron, check the extractor filter and swap in the box's spare if the running filter reads loaded, load the solder-wire spool; set out the 6 safety glasses at the station; pull the Day 4 burn kit out of the Day 4 box and set it beside the station, within 10 feet, per the safety plan's PPE line (the kit's staging ride is this bullet, so no second trip back to the box)
  • Pull the 4 markers off the equipment shelf and test them; the rotation order goes up on the wall with one of them during the demo, and the crew returns all 4 to the shelf at the handoff
  • Verify the laptop USB drivers; print the forecast sheet three times, one per team table (the print-ready file generated on the instructor laptop in the week-before window)
The weakness attackedAI has no senses. It can't take today's temperature, and it can't be trusted to guess it.
Deliverable · Working sensor station + overnight logged dataset, harvested and graphed at the Friday opening
Project block (self-directed, 45 min) · Handout: "The Sensor Read" (full text in §03b below). Write five sentences: "why fresh measured data beats a fluent prediction." Hand-graph the first 15 minutes of readings from this morning's deploy. Seniors compute the forecast error; juniors mark the high and low, then pin the hand-graphed sensor chart to the wall at the block's end. At block end, crew collects the 12 plastic graphing rulers from the stations back into the Day 4 box at the instructor table (prep laid them out from that box; this is the step that returns them), so the next session's Day 4 prep finds them where the checklist says they are, not scattered across the stations. Tonight's-low predictions, written on the overnight log chart issued at the handoff, get checked at Friday's opening harvest-and-graph.
Day 5 · Friday

Fabrication Friday

0:00-0:10Harvest + the challenge. The sensor charts pinned Thursday come off the wall: each camper presses button A on their station at the overnight row and reads the overnight low off the LED scroll, the whole row read in parallel in about two minutes, then checks whether their Day 4 low-prediction held and marks the prediction-held circle on the overnight log chart, five minutes, all twelve in parallel at the overnight row, and writes the measured overnight low on the same chart. The 12 overnight log charts (each camper's tonight's-low prediction card, issued at Thursday's handoff) are collected at the same time; both charts file into the portfolio folder on the shelf, the prediction card behind the sensor chart, so Day 9's assembly pulls the sensor chart from there. Then the challenge: show the AI-designed phone stand, prompted and printed earlier in the week by the instructor. The rule of the day: no edits to the AI's file. It printed exactly as prompted.
0:10-0:40Test lab. Teams stress-test the prints: drop test, phone fit test, 500g load test. Log every failure on the failure log sheet with a cause guess: tolerance, thin wall, or fantasy geometry (a feature that cannot exist in the real world).
0:40-0:55Redesign. Fix exactly one failure in CAD. Juniors use guided templates (thicken the wall, add a fillet); seniors model freeform. As each pair finishes, the instructor photographs the fixed design on screen with the print-station camera: that photo is the "after" half of the pair's photo pair. During the project block, the instructor queues the fixed files to print and pins one slip per pair on the queue board: pair names, filename, queue time.
0:55-1:00Handoff. Before-and-after portfolio pages issued, one resealable bag and one captions pen per camper with the page. Crew photographs each camper's failed print with the print-station camera: that photo is the "before" half of the photo pair. The prints stay with their owners into the project block; each one is bagged and labeled for the museum shelf at the block's 0:40-0:45 museum step. The fixed files print Friday afternoon on the two enclosed printers; the instructor pulls each finished revision off the bed once it reads under 50C and shelves it on the museum shelf beside its pair's failed print, so the physical before-and-after sits side by side for the showcase. Crew returns the 12 sensor stations to the labeled charging tray.
If it runs longCAD redesign gets 15 minutes, no more. If a team stalls, the instructor hands them the finished pre-fixed template file from the Day 5 box (copied onto the stick at Session Zero Hour 3 from the week-before build) and the before-and-after page still ships. The instructor queues fixed files for printing during the project block either way.
Materials, 12 campers, pre-kitted
  • 12 pre-printed AI designs (two sliced designs x six copies: printer 1 prints design A only, printer 2 prints design B only, two copies each per day Tue-Thu, printed by the instructor; enclosed printers run in the room during lessons and project blocks; finished prints shelved on the print shelf beside the printers as they come off the beds)
  • 12 failure log sheets (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6; live in the Day 5 box); 12 junior CAD templates (digital files, loaded on all 8 cart laptops at Session Zero Hour 3) + CAD access (seniors)
  • Test rig: 500g weight, drop mat, 3 phone dummies, plus 1 spare drop mat and 1 spare phone dummy (reusable shop stock; staged on the equipment shelf at Session Zero, about $45; the spares are the first claim on the test-rig wear share)
  • Queue board by the door (2x3 ft corkboard, reusable shop stock; staged on the equipment shelf at Session Zero, pulled in Day 5 prep, pinned by the door, returned to the shelf at the block's end) + a box of thumbtacks (reusable shop stock, same staging) + 6 queue slips (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6) and 6 blank claim tags, one of each per pair (the slips and tags live in the Day 5 box)
  • 12 clear resealable bags, one per failed print (live in the Day 5 box; bought on the Day 5 consumables line, about $8 for twelve; issued with the portfolio pages at the handoff for the block's 0:40-0:45 museum step)
  • Print-station camera (1 of the 3 field-kit cameras; the instructor photographs the fixed CAD designs with it during the redesign, the crew photographs the failed prints with it at the handoff)
  • 12 before-and-after portfolio pages (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6; live in the Day 5 box)
  • 12 dark felt-tip captions pens (reusable shop stock, about $12 for twelve, bought once inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line; live in the Day 5 box; issued with the portfolio pages at the handoff, one per camper)
  • 12 project-block handouts, "The Fix Page" (live in the Day 5 box; issued at the handoff)
Prep checklist, one person, about 30 min
  • Pull the 12 AI-design prints from the print shelf; inspect for failures; re-queue any failed print from the saved file during the Friday project block
  • Pull the test rig (500g weight, drop mat, 3 phone dummies) from the equipment shelf and lay it out; inspect the mat and dummies and replace a torn mat or cracked dummy from the spare drop mat and spare phone dummy staged beside the rig; open the CAD templates on the laptops
  • Pull the queue board and the thumbtack box off the equipment shelf and stand the board by the door; pull the queue slips, blank claim tags, the 12 before-and-after portfolio pages, the 12 resealable bags, the 12 captions pens, and the 12 failure log sheets from the Day 5 box; pin one blank claim tag per pair to the queue board by the door with the thumbtacks (the Fix Page handout's header and stuck protocol read the tags at the board, not at the printer); keep the 6 queue slips at the print station for the instructor's project-block queuing and queue-board pinning (the 0:40-0:55 redesign only photographs the finished designs on screen; the fixed files are queued and the slips pinned during the project block, because the slip carries the queue time), and set the 12 failure log sheets out at the team tables, one per camper, for the 0:10-0:40 test lab, and stage the bags, the pens, and the portfolio pages for the handoff issue
  • Pull 1 field-kit camera off the labeled charging tray; confirm the charge light; stage it at the print station (it photographs the fixed designs during the redesign and the failed prints at the handoff, and returns to the tray after the project block); pull one of the 3 USB SD card readers off the equipment shelf and stage it beside the printer (it reads the camera's card onto the instructor laptop for the 24 photo pages in the block's first minutes)
  • Check both printers' filament levels on the displays; if either spool reads under about one-quarter, swap in the mid-week spare from the equipment shelf before the Friday afternoon revision prints
  • Clear the failed-print museum shelf for Friday's additions
The weakness attackedAI describes objects it has never held. Physics doesn't read the prompt.
Deliverable · Failed AI print + fixed human revision, presented side by side
Project block (self-directed, 45 min) · Handout: "The Fix Page" (full text in §03b below). Mount the before-and-after photo pair with captions: "the AI did this; I fixed that." Verify your pair's queue slip on the queue board: no laptop needed, the instructor queues the fixed files from the lesson. At block end, crew collects the 12 captions pens from the stations back into the Day 5 box, unpins the 6 queue slips and any claim tags pinned at the 0:30 queue check off the queue board and files them back into the Day 5 box at the instructor table, so the next session's Day 5 prep finds the slips where the checklist says they are, not pinned to a board on the shelf, and returns the queue board and the thumbtack box to the equipment shelf, pulls the camera's SD card out of the USB SD card reader and re-seats it in the print-station camera (the Day 2 block-end crew step does the same for all three camera cards, so no teaching morning opens with a camera and its card in two places), and returns the print-station camera to the labeled charging tray, plugged in to charge (the Day 5 prep promises the return after the project block; this is the crew step that performs it, so the camera is back on its named home with a full charge for the next session); crew returns the USB SD card reader to the equipment shelf.

Week 2 · The Producer's Chair

Days 6–10 · direct the machine, own the outcome

Each day is one 60-minute instructor-led lesson with a minute-by-minute run-of-show, followed by a 45-minute self-directed project block campers complete from a printed handout, in the same room. The block needs no adult instruction: the handout, the answer key, and the wall rubric do the teaching, and the instructor stays in the room, silent, supervising. Every day also lists its exact materials and a prep checklist completable by one person in 30 minutes.

Day 6 · Monday

The Interview Room

0:00-0:10Open. AI Fail of the Day, then the mission brief: "Today you learn the question a machine can never ask." Question-craft sheets issued; the visiting guest is introduced, a community member briefed in advance, present for the Day 6 lesson, never alone with a camper.
0:10-0:35Question craft. Campers draft 8 interview questions on the sheet and test them with the AI chatbot on a laptop. Then three campers each put one question to the guest live, and the class compares what a real human actually said versus what the chatbot invented. Juniors write fact-finding questions; seniors write questions that earn a story.
0:35-0:55Recording rotation. Two waves, 8 minutes each. Wave 1: pairs A, B, and C record their 8-minute interviews, A with the guest at the center table, B and C running peer interviews from their question-craft sheets in two corners. D, E, and F sit in the observation arc around the guest table, each with a Moment Marker transcription card, live-marking the three marker moments (the correction, the laugh, the follow-up that earned the story) as A's guest interview unfolds. Then they swap: D interviews the guest in wave 2 while E and F record peer interviews, and A, B, and C take the observation arc to mark D's guest interview live. Wave 1 records on interview recorders 1 through 3 and wave 2 on recorders 4 through 6 (the 3 field-kit recorders plus the 3 extra recorder kits), one recorder per pair for the whole session, so each recorder holds exactly one 8-minute interview and every pair keeps its own recorder into the project block. The guest can only sit for two interviews, so the other four pairs conduct peer interviews; every pair records a full 8 minutes, and the two guest interviews go into the "Day 6 Showcase Interviews" shared album for the showcase (created at Session Zero on the camp photos account; the materials captain copies both files off recorders 1 and 4 in the named upload beat at the project block's end, so the showcase copy lands before any recorder leaves the room). The instructor circulates the whole time; all recording happens inside the room. Each camper keeps the same Moment Marker transcription card for the project block.
0:55-1:00Handoff. "Moment Marker" transcription handout issued. Each pair keeps its recorder through the project block for the listen-back. At block end, the materials captain runs the named upload beat first: plugs recorders 1 and 4 (the two guest-interview recorders) into the instructor laptop with the recorder USB cable and drags the two 8-minute files into the "Day 6 Showcase Interviews" shared album, named Day6-Guest-Interview-A and Day6-Guest-Interview-D, about 5 minutes; the instructor plays the first 10 seconds of each file in the album to confirm the copy. Then crew collects the 6 recorders, their splitters, and the labeled pair pouches to the labeled charging tray, and the instructor confirms all six charge lights before pickup pings. Each pair returns its recorder's lav mic to its kit's named home: the 3 field-kit lav mics go back into the 3 field kits on the equipment shelf and the 3 extra lav mics go into the extra recorder kits (labeled 'Extra 1' through 'Extra 3') beside the tray, so no lav mic is left loose at an interview corner and every kit is whole for the next session. No recorder ever leaves the room. The guest is thanked at the room door and shown out; the instructor stays in the room with the twelve campers, because the safety plan's line-of-sight rule has no exceptions. The HDMI cable and the USB-C-to-HDMI adapter unplug from the room screen and return to the equipment shelf. The recorder USB cable unplugs from the instructor laptop and returns to the equipment shelf (its Session Zero shop-stock home; prep pulled it from there for the upload beat), so the next session's Day 6 prep finds the cable where the checklist says it is, not a tangle at the laptop.
If it runs longSix recorders, one per pair, 8-minute interviews: first wave 8 minutes, a 2-minute swap, second wave 8 minutes, 2 minutes of slack. The swap is the only moving part: the next three pairs are at their stations (two corners plus the guest table) and the next observation arc is seated before the last three leave. If the guest is late, pair A does a peer interview in wave 1; the wave-1 observation arc rehearses its live-marking on that peer interview until the guest arrives, the guest sits with the next recording pair when they arrive, and the second guest interview moves to the top of the project block, led by the Moment Marker handout. The guest departs through the room door right after that interview, the instructor staying in the room.
Materials, 12 campers, pre-kitted
  • 6 interview recorders (the 3 field-kit recorders + the 3 extra recorder kits), each with lav mic and 2-way headphone splitter, charged at Session Zero and untouched since, one per pair
  • 1 labeled 24-port powered USB charging tray (bought once as reusable shop stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line, about $50; staged on the equipment shelf at Session Zero, stays plugged in all session; the 24 battery devices, 12 micro:bits, 3 field cameras, 6 recorders, 3 rovers, need 24 ports, so a 6-port tray was mathematically impossible; the 8 cart laptops charge on the laptop cart, not the tray; the field cameras charge on it overnight between their use days, and the 6 recorders land on it at the Day 6 handoff for the overnight charge)
  • 12 camp-kept earbuds in 6 labeled pair pouches (ride with the recorders from Session Zero: 3 pairs inside the field kits, 3 pairs in the extra recorder kits beside the labeled charging tray; pulled and paired in prep) + 12 alcohol-wipe packets (live in the Day 6 box, pulled in prep)
  • 12 question-craft sheets (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6) + 12 "Moment Marker" transcription cards (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6; live in the Day 6 box)
  • 1 pre-loaded "AI Fail of the Day" slide on the instructor laptop (the Day 6 open's five-minute teardown)
  • 1 HDMI cable (6 ft) + 1 USB-C-to-HDMI adapter (reusable shop stock; staged and signal-tested at Session Zero Hour 4; pulled from the equipment shelf in prep for the room-screen connection; unplugged and returned to the shelf at the handoff)
  • 1 USB cable matching the interview recorders' transfer port, staged on the equipment shelf as shop stock (pulled in prep); the instructor laptop holds the "Day 6 Showcase Interviews" album link bookmark (created in Session Zero Hour 3 on the camp photos account)
  • 1 pre-briefed community guest (present for the Day 6 lesson; departs through the room door after the final interview)
  • Laptops with chatbot tab open, content filter on
  • 12 project-block handouts, "Moment Marker" (live in the Day 6 box; issued at the handoff)
Prep checklist, one person, about 25 min
  • Confirm the guest's arrival window and their signed media release (briefed the week before; the release covers the recorded interview, the showcase album copy, and the parents' showcase playback)
  • Open the pre-loaded Day 6 "AI Fail of the Day" slide on the instructor laptop; pull the HDMI cable and the USB-C-to-HDMI adapter off the equipment shelf and plug the laptop into the room screen
  • Pull the 3 recorders out of the 3 field kits on the equipment shelf and pull the 3 extra recorder kits (the ones labeled 'Extra 1' through 'Extra 3') from beside the labeled charging tray; every recorder rides with its own lav mic, its own splitter, its two labeled camp earbuds, and its labeled pair pouch from Session Zero (3 pairs inside the field kits, 3 pairs in the extra recorder kits); check each recorder's battery indicator (full since Session Zero, nothing has used them); test the lav mics; confirm each recorder is still paired with its own lav mic, splitter, and two earbuds and test playback at low volume
  • Pull the recorder USB cable from the equipment shelf; open the "Day 6 Showcase Interviews" album on the instructor laptop and confirm the bookmark (about 1 minute of the 25-minute checklist)
  • Pull the question-craft sheets, the transcription cards, and the 12 alcohol-wipe packets from the Day 6 box; stage the wipe packets at the two interview corners and the guest table for the block's 0:45 earbud wipe-down
  • Set 2 interview corners (the guest table at the center table is the third interview station); open the chatbot tab on laptops
The weakness attackedAI's "empathy" is pattern-matched text. Trust is built in person, in a pause, with eye contact.
Deliverable · Recorded 8-minute oral-history interview + marked transcript of the three marker moments (about 90 seconds)
Project block (self-directed, 45 min) · Handout: "Moment Marker" (full text in §03b below). Transcribe the three marker moments (about 90 seconds of audio, roughly 15 to 20 lines on the card), mark the hesitations, the laughter, the follow-up that earned the story. The full 8 minutes is not the job; the markers are the skill. Write one paragraph: the moment the machine could never have gotten.
Day 7 · Tuesday

Robotics Gauntlet

0:00-0:10Open. The AI's predicted route for the course, printed and posted. The mission: beat the prediction with a real robot. Course rules, stopwatch demo, safety check on the shared kits.
0:10-0:40Program the wanderer. Teams of 4 share one rover kit (3 kits total). Each team codes, runs a trial, and debugs in rotation; the course never sits idle. When a rover flips or gets lost, the team logs the physical cause: wheel slip, sensor drift, tape that reflects wrong. Seniors add the autonomous waypoint extension.
0:40-0:55The gauntlet. Timed trials, two runs per team, the instructor times and calls the scores. After each team's pair of runs, one 60-second debug huddle: what failed in the real world that the simulation never saw?
0:55-1:00Handoff. "Crash Report" log sheet issued. Crew packs the 2 ramps and the pop-up tunnel back to the equipment room, the rover-course kit's tagged Session Zero home, and returns the cones bag, the packing-peanut bag, and the lane-tape roll to the Day 7 box at the instructor table (Session Zero Hour 2 packs them there and the Day 7 prep pulls them from there, so the next session's Day 7 prep finds them in the box, not in the equipment room); crew returns the 3 rover kits to the labeled charging tray for the overnight charge, the stopwatch to the equipment shelf, and the spare-battery case to the Day 7 box.
If it runs longSix timed runs at two minutes each fill 12 minutes; the three team huddles take 60 seconds each, three minutes. That is the whole 15-minute window, exactly, with no slack, so the instructor calls "runs only, save it for the huddle" the moment a run slips. If coding runs past 0:40, the gauntlet drops to one run per team: 3 runs (6 minutes) plus the three huddles (3 minutes) land at 9 minutes, comfortably inside the window, with no borrowing from the project block. The instructor never steals from the 0:55 handoff: pickup pings go out on time.
Materials, 12 campers, pre-kitted
  • 3 rover robotics kits, charged (one per team of 4)
  • Obstacle course: 2 ramps + 1 tunnel (the rover-course kit, staged in the equipment room at Session Zero), packing-peanut "swamp" (sealed bag in the Day 7 box), lane tape (roll in the Day 7 box), cones (bag in the Day 7 box)
  • 12 "Crash Report" debug log sheets (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6; live in the Day 7 box); 1 printed AI route prediction
  • Stopwatch (reusable shop stock; staged on the equipment shelf; returned to the shelf at the Day 7 handoff); spare batteries (one labeled 8-cell NiMH AA set, bought on the Day 7 consumables line, live in a labeled case in the Day 7 box; charged at Session Zero Hour 3, pulled in Day 7 prep, returned to the box at the handoff)
  • 12 project-block handouts, "Crash Report" (live in the Day 7 box; issued at the handoff)
Prep checklist, one person, about 25 min
  • Pull the stopwatch from the equipment shelf and the spare-battery case from the Day 7 box (confirm the set reads full); pull the 3 rover kits off the labeled charging tray (their named home since Session Zero, the tray stays plugged in all session) and confirm every charge light; test every sensor
  • Pull the rover-course kit (2 ramps, 1 tunnel) from the equipment room and the packing-peanut bag, the cones bag, and the lane-tape roll from the Day 7 box; build the course (ramps, tunnel, swamp); tape the lane lines (on a weather-pivot morning, build the same course on the makerspace floor with the same kit)
  • Print the AI route prediction (the print-ready file generated on the instructor laptop in the week-before window); pull the 12 debug log sheets from the Day 7 box
The weakness attackedAI reasons about space from text. It has never turned a corner, slipped on cardboard, or recovered from a flip.
Deliverable · Gauntlet completion time + debug log of every real-world fix
Project block (self-directed, 45 min) · Handout: "Crash Report" (full text in §03b below). For each failure, the physical cause and the fix, in the team's own words. Seniors sketch the autonomous waypoint plan for Day 9.
Day 8 · Wednesday

The Taste Panel

0:00-0:10Open. Revisit the Day 1 truth-rubric, now with seven days of evidence behind it. The brief: the cohort's full pile of work goes on the wall; only what passes the panel earns a showcase slot.
0:10-0:40Taste panel. One panel, all twelve: every camper puts their single best piece on the wall and gets 2.5 minutes, score, argue, defend, cut, twelve pieces in thirty minutes, the clock is the discipline. Each camper marks their own piece's scorecard as the panel argues, the PASS/CUT row for each rubric test and the one-line argue sentence, while the instructor moderates from the front, because the card is the verdict's only written record and the project block's handout starts from it. The instructor moderates the room from the front; no piece is discussed in a corner out of sight. Juniors read the scores aloud; seniors argue the counter-case. The visiting guest judge watches all twelve pieces and picks the weakest survivor; the judge's gambit runs in the first five minutes of the next block. If the panel holds, the rubric is real.
0:40-0:55The judge's gambit + QC gates. Fifteen minutes, in three fives. 0:40-0:45: the guest judge argues the weakest survivor should not have made the cut; the panel defends or concedes, and the verdict is recorded beside the piece. 0:45-0:50: each capstone team drafts its actual gate, "ships only if it passes X." 0:50-0:55: the class stress-tests two of the drafts aloud, 2.5 minutes each. The full gate writing happens in the project block; what leaves this block is the draft, the verdict, and the stress-test memory.
0:55-1:00Handoff. QC-gate page template issued. The instructor pulls the 12 panel scorecards off the wall and hands each camper their own piece's card with the template, because the block's handout opens with the card in hand and the block's 0:40-0:45 step hands it back. The guest judge is thanked at the room door and shown out; the instructor stays in the room with the twelve campers, because the safety plan's line-of-sight rule has no exceptions.
If it runs longIf the panel deadlocks on a piece, the instructor calls the cut at the 2.5-minute mark and the piece goes on the Wall of Almost with the guest judge's verdict written beside it. If the guest judge is absent, the instructor argues the gambit from the one-page Day 8 judge brief in the instructor folder and the weakest-survivor pick falls to the senior campers' counter-case team, so the panel closes with a recorded verdict either way. Two QC gates are stress-tested aloud no matter what; the rest are read by the instructor during the project block.
Materials, 12 campers, pre-kitted
  • The week's full pile of camper work, wall-mounted
  • 12 panel scorecards (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6; live in the Day 8 box); the Day 1 truth-rubric poster is already on the wall (hung in Day 1 prep, stays up all session), not in any day-box
  • 12 QC-gate page templates (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6; live in the Day 8 box); 4 markers (reusable shop stock; staged on the equipment shelf at Session Zero, pulled from the shelf in prep, returned to the shelf after the project block)
  • 1 painter's tape roll (reusable shop stock; staged on the equipment shelf at Session Zero Hour 4, pulled from the shelf in prep and staged by the truth-rubric poster wall for the QC-gate posting; the block's post step needs wall adhesive, and the tape gun + badge tape lives in the Day 10 box, locked in the closet during the block)
  • 1 pre-briefed guest judge (local maker or teacher)
  • 12 project-block handouts, "The QC Gate" (live in the Day 8 box; issued at the handoff)
Prep checklist, one person, about 20 min
  • Pull the Day 8 box out of the storage closet and stage it at the instructor table (packed and locked there at Session Zero; untouched since); mount the week's work on the wall; pull the 12 panel scorecards from the Day 8 box and hang them in a row on the wall, one scoring station per piece; each camper mounts their best piece beside a card at 0:10 and the piece's verdict is recorded on that card during its 2.5 minutes
  • Pull the QC-gate templates from the Day 8 box; pull the 4 markers off the equipment shelf and test them; pull the painter's tape roll off the equipment shelf and set it by the truth-rubric poster wall for the gate posting
  • Confirm the guest judge's arrival (briefed the week before); if the judge has not confirmed by 8:45 the morning of Day 8, run the judge-absent fallback in the "If it runs long" note above: the instructor argues the gambit from the one-page Day 8 judge brief in the instructor folder, and the weakest-survivor pick falls to the senior campers' counter-case team, so the panel closes with a recorded verdict either way and no second adult is needed
The weakness attackedAI averages all taste into mush. "Correct" is common; "good" is a judgment call someone has to make.
Deliverable · Written QC gate for each capstone + curated shortlist with panel scorecards
Project block (self-directed, 45 min) · Handout: "The QC Gate" (full text in §03b below). Finish the QC-gate page with pass/fail examples from today's panels. Juniors write the examples; seniors write the "what we almost let through" case study. At block end, every camper hands their panel scorecard back to the instructor; the instructor circles the taste-panel award winners on the twelve cards and files them back in the Day 8 box, which sits at the instructor table all day (pulled there in the morning prep; the QC-gate templates have left it for the wall), so the instructor never leaves the room mid-block and the Friday award-card writing copies a written record, not a two-day-old memory. Then the instructor returns the 4 markers and the painter's tape roll to the equipment shelf. The markers go back last, after the award-winner circling, since the circling is the day's final marker work, so the next session's Day 8 prep pulls them off the shelf where the checklist says they are, not from beside the poster wall. At the Day 8 dismissal, the instructor relocks the Day 8 box in the storage closet, because the Day 10 prep asserts it was relocked there at the Day 8 dismissal and no step between the block end and Friday touches the box, so Friday's prep pulls it out of the closet where the checklist says it is, not from beside the instructor table.
Day 9 · Thursday

Ship Day

0:00-0:10Open. The ship list, posted: every capstone artifact that must exist by tomorrow's showcase, with the owner and the evidence line. No new features after today, only finishing.
0:10-0:40Build sprint. Final capstone work in capstone teams: dataset story, printed object, interview piece, or rover demo. AI tools are allowed wherever they help and replaced wherever they fail; the instructor circulates and asks the Day 1 question: "What made you sure?"
0:40-0:55The credit roll. Each camper writes their one-page credit roll with their team: what the AI did, what the humans did, what failed, who fixed it. Every team member signs each one, so no contribution is unnamed. Accountability is the last page of every project.
0:55-1:00Handoff. Capstones are locked for showcase. Crew returns the 3 rover kits to the labeled charging tray for the overnight charge; the Day 10 prep pulls them for the rover-demo showcase stations. Take your portfolio folder at your seat: final assembly in the project block.
If it runs longAt 0:35 the instructor calls the scope cut: cut features, never the credit roll. Anything unfinished ships as a named known gap on the credit roll itself, signed anyway. The handoff at 0:55 is the lock: capstones are locked for showcase.
Materials, 12 campers, pre-kitted
  • Capstone materials per track (dataset files, prints, audio, rover kits)
  • 12 credit-roll templates, one per camper (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6; packed in the Day 9 box)
  • The 12 portfolio folders (issued on Day 1, living on the room's portfolio shelf all session; pulled off the shelf and staged one per seat in the Day 9 prep for the assembly block)
  • 3 pitch-rehearsal timers, one per team table (kitchen countdown timers; reusable shop stock, bought once inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line, about $15; kept in the Day 9 box, packed there at Session Zero Hour 2)
  • 2 ship lists, one posted and one spare (laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window; printed at Session Zero Hour 1, see Phase 6; live in the Day 9 box)
  • 12 project-block handouts, "The Credit Roll" (live in the Day 9 box; issued at the handoff)
Prep checklist, one person, about 25 min
  • Pull the 3 rover kits off the labeled charging tray; lay out each track's materials at its table; open the "Day 6 Showcase Interviews" album on one cart laptop, download the two guest interviews to it, and stage the laptop at the interview-piece capstone table (the audio named in the capstone materials line)
  • Pull the 2 ship lists (post one, keep one spare), the 12 credit-roll templates, and the 3 pitch-rehearsal timers from the Day 9 box; set one timer on each team table; stage one credit-roll template at each camper's seat beside the staged portfolio folder (the lesson's 0:40-0:55 credit-roll segment writes on the template, and the project-block handout's needs-line asks for it at the seat, so it needs a named home, not a pull with no destination)
  • Pull the 12 portfolio folders off the shelf; take the 12 QC gates off the wall (posted beside the truth-rubric poster on Day 8) and file each into its camper's folder; stage one folder at each camper's seat (the Day 9 handoff sends campers to their seats, not the shelf). As each QC gate comes off the wall, write that camper's artifact, owner first name, and evidence line onto the posted ship list, one line per gate: the gates name what ships and who owns it, so the ship list's content is written here, in prep, about five minutes of the 25-minute checklist, and the lesson's 0:00 open posts a list that is already filled in
  • Pull the Day 10 box out of the storage closet and stage it at the instructor table; lay the blank showcase program master on the table (tomorrow's kit staging, so the instructor never leaves the room during the project block)
The weakness attackedNo model can be responsible for an outcome. Campers learn responsibility is the feature, not the tax.
Deliverable · Shipped capstone + signed producer's credit roll
Project block (self-directed, 45 min) · Handout: "The Credit Roll" (full text in §03b below). Polish the credit roll, finish the portfolio folder, rehearse the 60-second pitch each camper gives at their showcase station tomorrow. At block end, crew returns the 12 assembled folders to the labeled portfolio shelf, so Friday's prep finds them. While the campers work, the instructor fills in the showcase program master at the instructor table, the Day 10 box staged there in prep: the twelve stations in pitch order, each with camper first name, capstone title, and track, from the posted ship list and today's pitch-rehearsal pairs. The first-names-only rule: anything another family can see (showcase programs, gallery labels, the mounted credit roll) carries first names only; full names appear only on take-home diplomas, the Airtable roster, and the pickup-authorization list. The instructor never leaves the room during the block; the filled-in master rides back in the Day 10 box, which returns to the storage closet after dismissal, so Friday's prep prints parent copies from a finished master, not a blank page.
Day 10 · Friday

Showcase

0:00-0:10Open. The gallery install plan: twelve stations, the Wall of Almost, the failed-print museum, sensor charts live. Crew roles assigned; the filled-in showcase program master and the printed parent copies are staged at the door table for the pickup handout.
0:10-0:40Gallery build. Campers install the exhibition. Every display explains the same thing: what the AI couldn't do, and who did it instead. The instructor is at the wall with the tape gun; nothing leaves the room.

0:55-1:00Handoff. Project block: final polish. The parents' showcase runs 11:15-11:45, the whole cohort present, the instructor with them the entire time, handing out the diplomas and taste-panel awards at the showcase. The station script: a parent who arrives fresh at your station gets the two-sentence intro off the back of your Moment Marker transcription card and then the 60-second pitch; a parent who arrives mid-pitch or with seconds only gets the three-sentence "Late arrivals" version from the inside front of your portfolio folder. Never read all three to one person; the script names which version fits the moment.
If it runs longThe instructor is at the wall with the tape gun: stations first, Wall of Almost second, failed-print museum last. If rehearsal runs past 0:55, pitches drop to 30 seconds each and the second fix round merges into the parents' showcase itself, which runs 11:15-11:45.

03b · The handout kit

Ten printed handouts. Zero instruction required.

The red team demanded handouts that exist, not handouts that are promised. Below is the full text of all ten: the one-page sheets campers work from in the 45-minute self-directed project block. Each names what the camper needs, what to do in 45 minutes, what to do if they finish early, and what to do if they get stuck. Every camper keeps a name-labeled portfolio folder on the room's portfolio shelf; each block ends with that day's artifact filed to its named home: the portfolio shelf (Days 2, 3, 5, 6, 7, and Day 9, when the assembled showcase portfolio goes back on the shelf for Friday's prep), the wall (Day 4's sensor chart, Day 8's QC gate; Day 4's deploy log sheet files to the portfolio shelf at the block's end), the camp journal (Day 1), or the backpack itself (Day 10, when the finished portfolio, journal, diploma, and award cards go home). The Day 1 handout teaches the scan-tab convention once, a sticky-note tab on a journal page that the instructor scans at pickup into the camper's QR-linked gallery, and the Day 1 lesson demos it at the flatbed scanner. Day 8's gates come off the wall into the folders at Day 9 prep, and Day 9's block assembles the full showcase portfolio from the folders prep staged at the seats (pulled off the shelf). Each also carries a perforated tear-off strip at the bottom: that day's dinner-table conversation card, the one question that rides home in the backpack. Nothing in any handout assumes a laptop; printed pages and the room's tools carry the whole block. The answer key for each sheet lives in the instructor folder, and the wall rubric answers the hard questions. The instructor stays in the room, silent, supervising, for the whole block. The silence rule binds the instructor, not the campers: the room runs on quiet voices, task words only, spoken to your partner or team and no one else, because five of the ten sheets (Days 3, 6, 8, 9, and 10) require campers to speak: the Day 3 team verdicts argued out loud, the Day 6 showcase intro read to a partner, the Day 8 gate drafts reviewed aloud, the Day 9 pitch rehearsed in pairs, and the Day 10 captions read aloud while neighbors help stations across the gallery. A handout that demands silence and speech at once cannot be followed, so those five sheets say so plainly and the other five stay silent.

Day 1 · project-block handout

Failure Autopsy

You need: the Day 1 journal page, the AI Fail binder at your team's table, your camp pen (staged at your seat with the journal on Day 1, kept all session). 45 minutes, silent work, same room. Room-only steps: the 0:00-0:10 teardown on the room screen. Everything else may finish at home in the journal. Scan-tab convention (taught once, used all session): a journal page you want in your QR-linked camp gallery gets a sticky-note scan-tab; the scan-tab pads live on the door table next to the flatbed scanner. At pickup, hand the journal to the instructor at the door; flagged pages scan at about 30 seconds a page into your gallery folder, and the journal goes back in your backpack.

  1. 0:00-0:10, watch. Play the pre-recorded teardown on the room screen. Part 1: a lawyer sanctioned after ChatGPT invented six court citations. Part 2: Google's AI answer telling people to put glue on pizza. Both real, both on the public record.
  2. 0:10-0:25, autopsy. Fill the table for each disaster: what happened / who trusted it / what got checked too late / your one-line rule.
  3. 0:25-0:40, add three. Your team's AI Fail binder holds twelve printed real-world blunders. Pick three and add each to your journal with one autopsy line.
  4. 0:40-0:45, sign. Finish the Day 1 items on your laminated Producer's Checklist and sign it. Crew returns the 3 AI Fail binders from the team tables to the Day 1 box at the instructor table, so the next session's Day 1 prep finds the binders where the checklist says they are, not loose on the tables.

Finished early? Write the "how it should have been checked" protocol, one page, as if you were the editor in charge. Seniors: write it so another ten-year-old could run it alone.

Stuck? Trade journals with a neighbor and compare autopsies. The wall rubric answers the hard questions.

Day 2 · project-block handout

The Labeler

You need: your printed 20-photo contact-sheet from this morning's safari (20 numbered thumbnails with label lines; this sheet is your label sheet), a red pen for QC marks. 45 minutes, silent work, same room. Room-only steps: the 0:20-0:30 QC swap (needs a partner) and the 0:40-0:45 shelf filing. Everything else may finish at home.

  1. 0:00-0:20, label. Label your 20 photos: species, angle, light, confidence (1 to 3). When confidence is 1, say why in one line.
  2. 0:20-0:30, QC swap. Trade sheets with a partner. Mark each missed edge case with the red pen, one per sheet, without erasing their work.
  3. 0:30-0:40, the paragraph. Write one paragraph: "what the model missed and why." Backlit leaves and damaged plants are the classic misses; explain the cause, not just the miss.
  4. 0:40-0:45, file. Return the red-pen sheet to its owner and file your labeled set in your portfolio folder on the shelf.

Finished early? Seniors: propose one new dataset rule the team adopts tomorrow, written as one sentence the whole class could follow. Juniors: relabel your three lowest-confidence photos with a second opinion.

Stuck? The Day 1 rubric poster has the "how do we know something is true" checklist. Work it line by line.

Day 3 · project-block handout

The Verdict Report

You need: your remaining-claim cards, the verdict template, this morning's field notes. 45 minutes, same room, quiet voices: speak only to your team, task words only. Room-only steps: the 0:15-0:40 team assembly and the 0:40-0:45 shelf filing. The verdict drafting may finish at home with your verdict template (the two claim lines carry the claims; this morning's field notes carry the evidence); the claim cards stay with the team in the room.

  1. 0:00-0:15, verdicts. Write verdicts on two claims each: take two of your team's claim cards (the team divides its five claims so every claim is taken by at least one camper; doubling up with a teammate on purpose is allowed, and the disagreement gets settled next) and write VERIFIED, BUSTED, or WE COULDN'T CONFIRM IT in the two claim blocks on your template. Every verdict needs an evidence line: photo, measurement, or a named primary source.
  2. 0:15-0:30, team assembly. Merge your verdicts onto the team report: the team picks one member's template as the team report sheet and copies the team's two official verdicts onto its two claim blocks, claim, verdict, evidence line, and source line (the two claims the team most wants to stand behind, usually the most contested ones). Disagree with each other out loud; the report records the disagreement and how you settled it.
  3. 0:30-0:40, the appendix. For every "couldn't confirm it": what would a second check cost? Time, money, access, one line each, in the appendix box on your template.
  4. 0:40-0:45, sign. Every team member signs the team report. File your own template in your portfolio folder on the shelf; the signed team report rides back in the Day 3 box at the instructor table. Crew collects the 15 claim cards from the team tables back into the Day 3 box at the instructor table, stacked behind the three signed team reports, so the day's evidence set is complete and the consumable cards, reprinted each session on the Day 3 consumables line, are accounted for before they recycle.

Finished early? Pick the weakest VERIFIED in the room and attack it. If it survives, it deserves the signature.

Stuck? The phrase of the day is "we couldn't confirm it." That is a complete verdict, not a failure, as long as the evidence line says what you tried.

Day 4 · project-block handout

The Sensor Read

You need: your deploy log sheet and graph chart from this morning's deploy (your sensor kit at your seat), the forecast sheet for your team table (one per table, shared by your team), a ruler (the clear 12-inch one in your sensor kit at your seat). 45 minutes, silent work, same room. Room-only steps: pinning the finished chart to the wall and filing the deploy log sheet at 0:45. Everything else may finish at home.

  1. 0:00-0:10, read. Read this morning's log, the first 15 minutes of readings from your deploy. Mark the high and the low with the ruler; write the range in the corner of the chart.
  2. 0:10-0:25, graph. Hand-graph those 15 minutes of readings. Label both axes; a graph with no labels is a guess with decoration.
  3. 0:25-0:35, five sentences. Write five sentences on "why fresh measured data beats a fluent prediction." Sentence 1 starts with a number you measured. Sentence 5 says exactly what the forecast got wrong.
  4. 0:35-0:45, compare, pin, and file. Seniors: compute the forecast error at three timestamps (measured minus forecast). Juniors: mark the 15 minutes where the sensor and the forecast disagreed most, and write one line about why. Pin the finished chart to the wall before you leave: Friday's harvest starts with it. File your deploy log sheet in your portfolio folder on the shelf: the raw readings filed behind the finished chart, the same raw-behind-product rule the Day 2 tally sheets follow.

Finished early? Predict tonight's low from your own data, write it down, and check it tomorrow morning. A wrong prediction in your own handwriting is worth a hundred right ones from a model.

Stuck? Copy the axis labels from the sample chart pinned by the door. Then plot just three points. The rest is the same motion.

Day 5 · project-block handout

The Fix Page

You need: the before-and-after portfolio page, your failure log sheet from this morning's test lab (staged at the team tables in prep, one per camper), your photo pair from the print station (your failed-print photo plus your pair's fixed-design photo, printed by the instructor in the block's first minutes), the captions pen, and one resealable bag (issued with the portfolio page at the handoff, for the 0:40-0:45 museum step). The queue board by the door holds one blank claim tag per pair. 45 minutes, silent work, same room. Room-only steps: the 0:30-0:40 queue-board check, the museum bagging, and the shelf filing at 0:40-0:45. The mounting and captions may finish at home.
