  • Lightning mid-mission: the walk-out abort ruleThe weather call above covers heat and AQI at 8 AM, but a thunderstorm can build after the call, and no page rule said what happens when thunder reaches a group of twelve campers already in the field with one adult. The rule is fixed and printed on a laminated abort card in the field bag: if thunder is heard or lightning is seen, the mission aborts. No judgment call, no debate with twelve witnesses: the instructor calls "Thunder, buddies, back," buddy pairs form on the spot, the count runs aloud, and the group walks single file straight to the venue along the mapped route, no lingering under trees, no waiting it out in the open. The abort move is always walk out, never wait out: the instructor keeps the group moving on the mapped route toward the nearest hard shelter marked at the T-2 site visit, line of sight unbroken, and the water-case bottles come with the group because the walk-back count already covers the empties. Prevention rides the 9:15 walk-out count the instructor already staffs: two minutes at the door table, the instructor checks the radar on their own phone, and a National Weather Service severe-thunderstorm warning over the venue or route at walk-out moves the day to the mapped indoor alternate, the same way the 8 AM heat call does. After an abort, the group does not step out again until 30 minutes after the last thunder, counted from the inside; if that wait outlives the mission block, the pre-built indoor pivots below absorb the rest of the day because every pivot is Session Zero pre-built. Every abort logs to the Airtable incident log with a timestamp, and the parent ping on the camp number carries one line ("field mission came in early for lightning, campers inside, day continues indoors"). No budget-line changes: the two abort cards are paper, laminated at Hour 2 with the other cards, folded into the $150 snacks-and-prints operating line. No timing changes: the radar check rides the walk-out count.
  • Heat illness on the walk: shade, water, the ten-minute sit, the walk-out ruleThe 8 AM call cancels the walk over 95F, but heat illness does not need 95F: heavy sweating, headache, dizziness, nausea, or cramps mid-walk is the named case each field-mission water case was bought for. Prevention rides the 9:15 walk-out count the instructor already staffs: one pump bottle of sunscreen sits at the door table for self-apply before the count (staged at Session Zero Hour 4 as reusable shop stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line; the instructor never applies sunscreen to a camper, and one adult means the bottle is pump-only, no hands on campers). Each camper keeps the one issued bottle for the whole walk, and the remaining twelve bottles of the case ride in the field bag side sleeve as spares, so a camper who sweats through the first bottle gets a second without breaking the walk-back count. The signs ladder is printed on the back of the two laminated abort cards from the lightning rule, so the Session Zero Hour 1 print run does not grow: heavy sweating, headache, dizziness, nausea, or cramps means the camper sits in the shade at the instructor's side immediately, drinks water, and gets a ten-minute sit while the group keeps working within the same sightline (the buddy stays with the pair, nobody leaves the sightline, the instructor never stops watching the group); confusion, slurred speech, hot skin without sweat, or fainting skips the ladder and fires the Tier 3 chain, 911 first, shade and water on the skin while the paramedics come. If the ten-minute sit does not bring the camper back, the walk aborts early the same way a lightning abort ends: the aloud count runs, the group walks out along the mapped route, and the parent pickup ping goes out on the camp number. Every heat episode logs to the Airtable incident log with a timestamp, and a parent who prefers their child to carry personal sunscreen adds it to the enrollment accommodation note at no charge.
  • Guests and judgesAny guest (interview subject, taste-panel judge, showcase attendee) is pre-briefed on the single rule: you are never alone with a camper. Guests stay in the main room, in the instructor's line of sight, for the duration of their visit. The Day 6 guest sits at a table in the middle of the room; recording pairs rotate through, the room never empties around the guest.
  • Personal devices: phones into the pouch at check-inCamper phones are the one thing the one-adult model cannot absorb mid-day. Twelve 10-to-14-year-olds with live phones means a field-mission walker filming instead of counting in, a lesson where the room screen competes with twelve notifications, and photos and video of other campers taken by children whose families may never have signed the media release, outside the DocuSign chain the safety plan already carries. So the rule is total and mechanical: personal phones go off or silent into a name-labeled resealable pouch at the door table during the 9:15 QR check-in; the twelve pouches lock in the combination-lock door-table lockbox (its combination is set at Session Zero Hour 4, held by the instructor only, and distinct from the med-lockbox and cooler-bag codes); the phones come out only at the 11:15 pickup sign-out, pouch handed back at the door with the backpack. The enrollment Typeform carries the phone-policy acceptance beside the med authorization and the pickup-authorization list, so no family meets this rule for the first time at the door. Parent-to-camper contact during camp hours goes through the camp number printed on the pickup-authorization list, the same channel as every Twilio parent ping; the Day 1 walk tells every camper the number. On field missions no personal phone leaves the venue: the three field-kit cameras are the camp's only cameras, which is exactly the media-release boundary the guests-and-judges bullet draws. The only exception is a phone that is itself a medical device (a phone paired to a glucose monitor), which stays on the camper under the parent-signed med authorization form; the rest of the rule still applies to it. A phone seen out during lessons goes to the instructor table in the open, the lesson never stops to fetch it, and the pickup is logged in the Airtable incident log with a timestamp; the device returns at the door table at pickup. No second adult is ever needed, because collection and return ride the check-in and pickup rows the instructor already staffs. Twelve name-labeled resealable phone pouches and the combination-lock door-table lockbox are bought once as reusable shop stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line (about $12), staged at Session Zero Hour 4 on the door table.
  • MedicationsEvery med needs a parent-signed authorization form (name, dose, time, storage) collected at enrollment through Typeform. All medications live in a labeled combination-lock lockbox held by the instructor (the med lockbox: its combination is set at Session Zero Hour 4, held by the instructor only, and distinct from the door-table lockbox and cooler-bag codes), not in camper bags; every dose is logged with time and the instructor's initials beside the words in the Airtable med log, visible to the parent at pickup. EpiPens and rescue inhalers ride in the instructor's field bag during field missions and sit on the instructor's table during lessons. An epinephrine auto-injector is administered by the instructor under the camper's parent-signed authorization form (the form names the device and the technique); the instructor rehearses with a trainer pen at Session Zero and keeps the trainer pen in the field bag. The trainer pen is one reusable epinephrine auto-injector trainer, about $15, bought once as reusable shop stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line and staged with the med lockbox at Session Zero Hour 4. Refrigerated meds go in a small lockable medication cooler bag (combination lock, code set at Session Zero Hour 4, held by the instructor only, and distinct from the door-table lockbox and med-lockbox codes) with a frozen ice pack inside, staged at Session Zero. Two reusable gel ice packs rotate through the bag: each evening the day's thawed pack goes into the instructor's home freezer when the bag leaves the venue, and each 8:45 prep the frozen pack goes back in the cooler bag before the drop-off thermometer-strip check, so the bag never opens the day on a thawed pack. The bag sits with the med lockbox at the instructor's table during lessons and rides in the instructor's field bag on field missions. Pack the instructor's field bag with its full session contents, exactly: the labeled med lockbox, the EpiPens and rescue inhalers, the epinephrine trainer pen, the lockable medication cooler bag with its frozen ice pack, the camp first-aid kit, the instructor folder, the instructor's phone and the instructor medical card in the bag's named zip phone pocket, the field bag's abort card and the route card in the bag's front pocket (the spare abort card files in the instructor folder at Session Zero Hour 4, per the Hour 1 print list, so the front pocket never holds both; the route card prints at Day 3 prep, so the front pocket carries only the abort card on the Day 2 safari), and the laminated allergy action plans clipped to the bag's outside panel. On field-mission mornings one full 24-bottle water case goes in the bag's side sleeve before the instructor counts the bag's contents against the Session Zero Hour 4 list, so the morning count catches a full case like everything else; the Day 2 safari draws the first case, the Day 3 verification walk draws the second, and the spent case leaves the shelf after the Day 2 walk-back count. The same morning motion moves the instructor's phone into the bag's zip phone pocket after the 8:45 inbox read, then moves the instructor medical card from its table clip into the pocket beside the phone, and the walk-back count returns the card to the table clip and the phone to the instructor table beside the clip, so the room script's clip line, the Day 2 walk-out rehearsal's pocket line, and the field script's pocket line all name the same card in its right home. The bag's named home is the equipment shelf beside the med lockbox, and it rides with the instructor on every field mission, so no medication, authorization paper, or answer key ever sits in a day-box or in a camper's reach unsupervised. The bag is carried in and out by the instructor each day and is never left at the venue overnight; a stick-on thermometer strip is checked at drop-off and logged in the same Airtable med log with the dose. The instructor does not administer anything beyond the label's instructions; anything ambiguous gets a call to the parent before the dose.
  • Scheduled midday dose on a field walk: the dose gate, the buddy witness, the shielded code.Any dose whose time falls inside a field-walk window is known at enrollment from the parent-signed authorization form, and the med/allergy sheet in the folder that rides the field bag lists it. Prevention is mapped, not improvised: at the same T-2 venue walk-through that maps the routes and checks accessibility, the instructor marks one dose gate per route, a bench or shade point off the road where twelve can sit inside one sightline, and writes it into the launch plan beside the route card's 911 location line (the route card itself does not print until Day 3 prep); the route brief on a dose day names it matter-of-factly ("we sit at the bench for a few minutes"), no announcement of whose dose it is. At the gate the group sits off the road in buddy pairs inside the sightline on the illness bullet's sit form, a planned pause, never the "Freeze, buddies" emergency call. The dosing camper comes to the instructor's side with their buddy, who stands as the accountability witness (the same witness the lost-camper bullet names), inside the group's sightline, watching the dose and the log, never handling medication. The instructor opens the lockbox or the cooler bag with body and bag shielding the combination code from the group: the cooler bag's thermometer strip is read first and logged with the dose, the bag opens for seconds, dose out per the label and the authorization form, re-locked and zipped back into the field bag before the group stands; a water bottle from the side-sleeve case covers a dose that needs water, the empty rejoining the walk-back count. The dose is logged in the Airtable med log with time and the instructor's initials beside the words on the phone from the zip phone pocket right there at the bench, the same authorization-and-log chain the room runs. If anything is ambiguous the room rule holds on the route: call the parent before the dose, the group seated meanwhile. After the dose the aloud count resumes and the walk continues: no split, no shop entry, no one leaves the group. A restroom need at the gate still aborts the walk per the restroom bullet; a dose that comes back up hands the script to the illness-on-a-walk bullet.
  • Mid-session medication changes: the re-sign, the swap, the same-hour log.The med chain's four documents (the parent-signed authorization form, the med/allergy sheet, the lockbox contents, and the Airtable med log) can all go stale mid-session: a parent changes a dose, adds a new prescription, or discontinues a medication. The rule is flat: nothing enters or leaves the med chain on a verbal say-so, and no dose is given under a changed instruction until a fresh parent-signed authorization form is in the instructor's hand. The parent sends the change over the camp number the evening before (text or email through the camp number, never a verbal instruction at the door table); the instructor acknowledges in the 8:45 morning read, the overnight inbox check that opens the prep window every teaching morning, so the change never lands as a surprise during the 9:15 check-in rush. The new authorization form arrives the same morning, either a fresh DocuSign or a paper form signed at the door table at 9:15 with the group already in the U and the instructor never leaving the room; a dose under the changed instruction is not given until the signed form exists. The swap runs at the 9:15 prep window the instructor already staffs: the instructor updates the med/allergy sheet in the instructor folder, swaps the lockbox contents (the old medication is sealed in its original bag, initialed, and handed back to the parent at the 11:15 pickup the same day, never left at the venue overnight), and writes the change into the Airtable med log with time and the instructor's initials beside the words (old dose, new dose, the parent's name, "form re-signed"), the same log every dose already writes to. A discontinued medication is handed back at pickup and its row on the med/allergy sheet is struck through, initialed, and dated; a dose time that moves (a new midday dose on a field-walk day) is added to the med/allergy sheet and, where it applies, to the dose-gate brief the bullet above names, so the walk-out count knows about it. An increased dose or a brand-new prescription takes effect only after the signed form lands: a morning dose that falls before the form arrives is missed, and the miss is logged with the reason ("authorization pending"), never approximated by giving the old dose. A parent who discovers a change too late for the evening notice sends it with the camper to the door table with a signed paper form: the swap still runs at the 9:15 prep window, and the first dose under the new instruction follows the form, not the verbal. The pharmacy label on the new bottle is checked against the new form before the first dose; a label that does not match the form means the parent is called before anything is given, the room rule the dose-gate bullet already holds. The four-document chain needs no audio slot: a voicemail that names a med change is notice only (the form script above runs on the reply), and the Airtable med log's line for that dose names the notice channel ("notice by voicemail, [time]"), which is the audio's paper trail. A voicemail that contradicts the signed form in the folder loses to the form: the parent is called before anything is given, the same room rule the label-mismatch line already holds, and no dose runs under the voicemail's instruction until a re-signed form exists. No budget-line changes: the DocuSign envelope and the paper form sit inside the launch plan's existing documents; no new materials, no new prints beyond the one form. No timing changes: the swap rides the 9:15 prep window the check-in row already staffs.
  • The camp number after hours: the night's boundary, the 8:45 morning read, the named reply.The camp number is a Twilio SMS and voice line routed to the instructor's phone, provisioned at T-10w, routed and dry-run at T-6w, and three of the safety plan's rules already lean on it: the pickup-change rule, the med-change rule, and every Twilio parent ping. None of them named who reads the line after dismissal, so the first overnight message would have landed on an unread phone. The boundary is now fixed: after the last camper is collected at the 11:15 pickup, the camp number is not monitored until the next teaching morning. Parents learn this before paying: the enrollment confirmation states the hours line beside the 24-hour illness rule, the pickup-authorization list carries the camp number in its footer with the same line, and the week-of logistics pack repeats it: camp hours are 9:15 to 11:15, and messages sent overnight are read first thing each teaching morning. A true after-hours emergency is not a camp emergency: a camper home with family is a family 911 call, and anything for the next camp day waits for the morning read, because there is no second adult to staff a night desk and the instructor's off hours are off. The morning read opens the 8:45 prep window every teaching morning, about five minutes, on the instructor's own phone: every SMS and voicemail that landed since dismissal is read, and each parent message gets a "got it" reply on the camp number, so the family has a receipt before the doors open. Anything needing action is routed before the door table opens: a med change hands the script to the med-change bullet (the form-before-the-dose rule runs on the reply), a pickup change is checked against the pickup-authorization list (a same-day door-table change still gets a signature on the list, never a verbal), and an absence is written onto the paper attendance sheet, so the 9:15 count already knows the no-show. A message that lands after the morning read but before doors is read at the 9:15 check-in row the instructor already staffs, the same minute-block boundary the late-arrival rule runs on. Voicemails get a handling rule of their own, because the read promises to listen to every voicemail but no line said how long one lives on the account or what one authorizes. Every voicemail lives on the camp's Twilio account, never on the instructor's personal phone, and the Twilio voice configuration is hour-aware: a call inside camp hours rings the instructor's phone for about 20 seconds and an unanswered call hands to the camp's own Twilio voicemail greeting, so a parent's message never sits in a personal inbox the 8:45 read does not open, while a call outside camp hours skips the ring entirely and goes straight to the camp voicemail greeting, so the "not monitored" boundary is a routing fact and the instructor's phone never rings overnight; the camp's voicemail greeting states the hours line to the caller first. The Studio flow's date schedule holds the ten teaching dates (minus any sick-cancelled day, which the sick-day bullet pulls off the schedule the morning it cancels; a mid-lesson incapacitation keeps the date on the schedule while the script's Do Not Disturb step keeps the phone from ringing, because nobody can reach the Twilio console mid-crisis), entered at the T-6w build and re-verified at the T-2w step; the Say widget's greeting TTS text is read back at the same T-2w step, so the hours line the dry run heard is confirmed again before the first parent call; a calendar change in planning re-enters the dates before the first affected day, so the hour-aware ring rule always matches the real teaching day. The T-6w dry run proves both paths and both branches before launch, the test SMS answered from the same thread, the in-hours test call's voicemail played back from the Twilio console and deleted, and an after-hours test call confirming the phone never rings before the voicemail lands, so the retention rule's delete step and the silent overnight are practiced actions, not first tries on live messages. A voicemail is notice only, never authorization: a voicemail naming a med change hands the script to the med-change bullet (the reply asks for the re-signed form, and the med-change bullet's contradiction rule settles any conflict with the signed form), and a voicemail naming a pickup change is checked against the pickup-authorization list (the list holds until a change is signed on it, the same as a verbal change at the door table). A voicemail that contradicts the signed list loses to the list: the instructor calls the parent, replies on the camp number asking for a re-signed authorization, and nothing changes until the re-signed form lands. Retention is the camp's own rule, not Twilio's vendor default: a voicemail that names a med or pickup change is kept on the camp's Twilio account until the change it names is resolved and written into the log, then deleted at the next morning read; every other voicemail is deleted in the same morning read after its "got it" reply, so no voicemail lives on the account longer than it is needed. No budget-line changes: the Twilio parent-comms line, the camp number, and the instructor's phone are bought and provisioned stock. No timing changes: about five minutes inside the existing 30-minute prep the instructor's paid hours already cover.
  • Allergies and foodThe room is nut-aware: no nuts in snacks, all snack ingredients carry labels, the ingredient list is in the week-of logistics pack. The Day 8 "taste panel" is a critique panel of camper work, not a food tasting: the only food at camp is the shelf-stable snacks bought in the week-before window. Any camper with a food allergy on file gets their snack portion served first, from sealed packages, before shared surfaces touch it. Allergy action plans from parents are printed, laminated, and clipped to the instructor's field bag, next to the EpiPens.
  • Accommodations and access, one adultThe camp enrolls from the general public, so the plan names what one adult can support and what it cannot, before any family pays. The Typeform intake asks the accommodation question beside the med authorization and the pickup-authorization list: IEP or 504 plan, ADHD, autism or sensory needs, dyslexia or low vision, mobility, or anything else the family wants the instructor to know. Every requesting family gets a 15-minute accommodation call with the operator in the week before the session (T-1, operator labor like the other week-before tasks, outside the instructor's paid hours); the call ends with a written agreement, one page per camper, filed in the instructor folder at Session Zero Hour 4 beside the pickup-authorization list and the med/allergy sheet, so the instructor teaches from paper, not memory. Named supports, all engineered for the one-adult line-of-sight model: large-print handouts, the ten week-before layout files reprinted at 18pt, riding the Hour 1 print run at about 10 extra sheets per requesting camper, inside the run's margin; seating, the U's two end seats carry extra space and a standing option, no separate table, because no camper sits outside the instructor's sightline; sensory, fidget tools from shop stock at the camper's own seat and noise-reducing earplugs for the loud blocks (the Day 5 test lab, the Day 7 gauntlet), with self-directed sensory breaks at the camper's own table, never a separate room, because there is no second adult to staff one; reading, the day's handout steps read aloud to the whole room at the 0:55 handoff, about 60 seconds, and the five quiet-voice project blocks (Days 3, 6, 8, 9, 10) already permit task-words speech to a partner, so a buddy can re-read a step on request without breaking the silence rule the other five days hold. Field missions: route accessibility is checked at the same T-2 venue walk-through that maps the route; a route the whole cohort cannot walk is a route nobody walks, and the mapped indoor alternate runs instead, because the single-group rule has no accessible-route exception that needs a second adult. Honest limits, stated at enrollment: the camp cannot provide 1:1 aides, cannot modify the 1:12 line-of-sight model, cannot run a separate quiet room, and cannot deliver behavioral interventions beyond the published three-rule room contract. If the accommodation call finds the camp cannot support a camper safely, the family hears that before paying, and an enrolled family in the same position gets a full Stripe refund on the tuition collection and refunds API the launch plan already carries. Midday ADHD meds ride the existing med authorization and med-log chain; no new procedure. No budget-line changes: large print is venue-printer paper inside the $150 snacks-and-prints operating line; fidget tools and earplugs are reusable shop stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line.
  • Incident handlingThree tiers. Tier 1 (bandaid level: paper cut, blister): treated on the spot, logged in the Airtable incident log with a timestamp. Tier 2 (beyond a bandaid: burn, deeper cut, twisted ankle, allergic reaction without airway involvement): first aid from the camp first-aid kit, parent pinged via Twilio within 15 minutes, incident report form filled the same hour. Tier 3 (head injury, suspected concussion, lost camper): 911 first, then the parent call, then the incident form. Tier 3 (airway involvement / suspected anaphylaxis): the instructor administers the camper's prescribed epinephrine auto-injector per the authorization form first, then calls 911, then the parent, then the incident form. Group freeze governs every incident while one camper is being treated: the other eleven sit in the U with hands off tools (the iron back in its stand with the power off, craft-knife blades retracted, weights off the mats), buddy pairs check each other in, and treatment happens at the instructor table inside the room, where the first-aid kit already sits, so line of sight never breaks. Nothing resumes until the instructor counts twelve. Every incident, including Tier 1, gets a timestamped log entry; the log is exported to the parent at session end. The camp's one-adult design means no one chases a runaway while 11 wait: a camper who leaves the room, or who walks away from the group on a field walk, triggers Tier 3 immediately, the instructor calls 911 and counts the group. The form is one page, printed blank in Session Zero Hour 1: 12 copies filed in the instructor folder at Hour 4, so it is on the instructor table during lessons and in the field bag on field walks. Its fields are fixed: date, time, tier, location (room station, or the route name with the nearest landmark), the camper's full name (a formal safety record handed to the child's own parent at pickup, extending the full-name exception the Day 9 rule grants the take-home diplomas, the Airtable roster, and the pickup-authorization list), what happened, first aid given, the name of the parent contacted and the time of the Twilio ping, the 911 call time if any, the buddy witness's first name, the instructor's initials, and the follow-up needed. 'Filled the same hour' runs at the next block transition, never mid-lesson: the group settles into the next counted block with hands-off tools, the instructor counts twelve aloud, then fills the form at the instructor table in line of sight, so the count is never unwound to do paperwork. The parent of the involved camper signs the form at pickup; the timestamped entry goes into the Airtable incident log when the group is back at the venue (the paper log sheet is the tech-outage backstop); completed forms stay in the instructor folder through move-out.
  • Camper illness mid-day: one adult, no side roomWhen a camper reports nausea, headache, or a feverish feeling, or vomits, the sick-camper script runs, engineered so the instructor never leaves the room and line of sight never breaks. The sick camper moves to the sick chair (an ordinary chair pulled beside the instructor's table), stays inside the instructor's sightline with the other eleven, and no one leaves the building. The instructor takes the temperature with the sick kit's digital thermometer (100.4 F / 38 C or higher counts as fever) while the other eleven keep working, so no teaching minute is lost. A fever or any vomiting means same-day parent pickup: the instructor sends the pickup message over the camp number, the same Twilio chain that carries the 9:15 and 11:15 pings, and the camper rests in the sick chair with a water bottle until a listed adult arrives, expected within 30 minutes. If no parent or emergency contact answers within 30 minutes, the instructor works the pickup-authorization list top to bottom; the camper stays in the sick chair, resting, until an authorized adult collects them. Headache or stomach ache without fever or vomiting gets water, the sick chair, and twenty minutes: if it passes, the camper rejoins the group; if it does not, the parent gets the same pickup message. Vomit on the floor gets the two-minute spill treatment (gloves on, absorbent pads down, the mess bagged in the sick kit's zip bags, disinfectant wipes across the surface) during a natural pause in the lesson while the room keeps working. A camper who vomited or ran a fever may not return the next day unless they have been symptom-free for 24 hours without medication, and the enrollment confirmation states the 24-hour rule before any parent pays. Every illness episode is logged in the Airtable incident log with a timestamp, the same log the Tier chain writes to. If symptoms escalate (airway involvement, signs of anaphylaxis, any injury riding with the illness), the Tier 2/Tier 3 chain takes over from the sick chair, and nothing in this bullet changes it. No budget-line changes: the sick kit is a new tray beside the camp first-aid kit (digital thermometer with probe covers, disposable gloves, absorbent pads, emesis bags, disinfectant wipes, zip bags), about $35 of reusable stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line, assembled and staged at Session Zero Hour 4 beside the first-aid kit on the equipment shelf; it sits on the instructor table during lessons and rides inside the camp first-aid kit's bag on field missions, so the exact field-bag manifest the meds bullet lists does not change.
  • Camper illness on a field walk: the sit, the abort, the carry-out ruleThe room sick script needs a room: a sick chair, an instructor table, a floor to treat, and none of them ride on a field walk. The kit does: the sick kit rides inside the first-aid kit's bag in the instructor's field bag on every field mission, so the emesis bags, the thermometer, the gloves, and the zip bags are in the instructor's hand on the route. Detection is the same trigger as the room script: a camper says they feel sick or vomits on the route. The instructor calls "Freeze, buddies" once, the group's field-freeze call, and the campers sit off the road in buddy pairs, inside the sightline, while the sick camper sits at the instructor's side. An emesis bag comes out of the first-aid bag immediately, and any used bag is zipped and stowed back in the first-aid kit's bag (the floor spill treatment belongs to the venue; the bag is the treatment on the route). The instructor takes the temperature right there with the sick kit's thermometer; the fever line is still 100.4 F / 38 C. The mild case rides the heat bullet's ten-minute rule: headache or nausea without fever or vomiting gets water and about ten minutes seated at the instructor's side, the rest of the group in sight; if it passes, the group resumes the walk with the buddy inside the sightline the whole time. Fever or any vomiting on the walk means same-day parent pickup, sent over the camp number from the field (the instructor's phone rides the field bag's named zip phone pocket), but the pickup happens at the venue: the instructor runs the lightning bullet's abort form, the aloud count, and the group walks out together along the mapped route, the sick camper at the instructor's side, no split, no shop entry. On return the camper rests at the venue until the listed adult arrives, expected within 30 minutes, run through the same top-to-bottom pickup-authorization ladder the room script runs, and the day continues indoors on the mapped alternate. A camper who cannot walk (cannot stand, fainting, cannot keep water down) skips the ladder and fires the Tier 3 chain, 911 first with the exact location read off the route card, the group holding together at the last point off the road, exactly the escalation the room script names. The 24-hour symptom-free return rule and the timestamped incident log hold unchanged. No budget-line changes: the sick kit is bought and rides the field bag already, and prevention rides the existing walk-out brief, which adds "say so right away if you feel sick on the walk" to the no-shame line the restroom rule already states at every walk-out.
  • Hospital transport: the instructor stays, the paperwork ridesTier 3 calls 911 first, and when the paramedics decide a camper goes to the emergency department, the rule is fixed: the instructor stays with the eleven and the camper rides with EMS. This is the only honest configuration under the one-adult model: the instructor cannot leave eleven children unsupervised, and paramedics do not take direction from a chaperone in the rig. The safety plan says this plainly at enrollment so no family is surprised by it. The parent call follows the 911 call (Tier 3 order, unchanged), and the parent meets the camper at the hospital, not at the camp: the parent's drive starts on the 911 call, so the parent and the ambulance arrive in the same window. If the parent cannot be reached, the instructor works the pickup-authorization list top to bottom while the camper still rides; the signed emergency-medical-treatment authorization (below) covers the gap, and the camp number keeps publishing the hospital name and address until a listed adult confirms they are en route. What rides with the camper is paper, prepared before anything goes wrong: Session Zero Hour 4 files a one-page hospital handoff sheet in the instructor folder beside the med/allergy sheet and the pickup-authorization list. The sheet names the nearest emergency department to the venue (mapped at the T-2 site visit, address and phone printed on the Hour 1 print run) and carries a handoff block the instructor fills in about thirty seconds: camper name, med and allergy summary copied from the med/allergy sheet, what happened, what was administered (epinephrine, meds from the med log). Two copies are printed: one goes with the paramedics, one stays in the folder. On field missions the sheet rides in the field bag inside the instructor folder, which the field-bag manifest already carries, so a Tier 3 on a walk has the sheet in hand with no day-box pull; the same two copies serve every mission, riding out and back with the folder, and the morning count's found-or-re-printed rule replaces a copy a paramedic transport consumes. Enrollment consent: the Typeform intake carries an emergency-medical-treatment authorization beside the med authorization and the pickup-authorization list, signed before payment: consent for paramedics and emergency-department staff to treat the camper when a parent cannot be reached in time, and explicit agreement that the instructor remains with the group and does not ride in the ambulance. The enrollment confirmation restates the rule in one line: "In an emergency the instructor stays with the other campers; a parent meets the child at the hospital." No budget-line changes: the handoff sheet is paper folded into the $150 snacks-and-prints operating line; the nearest-ER mapping rides the T-2 site visit that already walks the venue.
  • Emergency: fire, lockdown, lost camperThe venue's evacuation plan is walked with the instructor at the T-2 site visit and rehearsed with the campers in the first 10 minutes of Day 1: assembly point, headcount there; no re-entry until emergency personnel or venue staff give the all-clear. The Day 1 walk is an announced drill: the instructor ends it aloud at the assembly point before leading the group back inside, so the rehearsal never models re-entry during a real alarm. Lockdown: doors lock from inside, campers to the far wall, attendance called silently, Twilio message to parents queued but not sent until the all-clear (no panicked texts mid-incident). Lost camper drill: the group freezes, the instructor counts, the venue is searched inward from the last known position; if the camper is not found in 5 minutes, it becomes a Tier 3 call. That form is room-only: on a field walk there is no venue to search inward and the instructor cannot leave the group to search, so the field variant in the bullet below governs. Instructor incapacitation: if the instructor is hurt and cannot lead, the two oldest campers run the room by script, rehearsed in the same Day 1 walk: the two oldest campers present take the two named roles, the card's face-down spot beside the phone is shown in the walk, seniors where enrolled, because enrollment sets no age-mix floor and a cohort could be all juniors; everyone stays in the room, the door locks from inside, the oldest camper calls 911 from the instructor's phone (kept on the instructor table during lessons), but first switches the phone to Do Not Disturb, and the script names the locked-phone path because the phone is locked and the oldest camper does not know the passcode: Control Center on an iPhone, or the quick-settings shade on an Android, opens from the lock screen with no passcode, so the oldest camper taps Do Not Disturb on there, the T-6w-hardened Do Not Disturb that a redial cannot pierce (repeated calls off, no Emergency Bypass contacts), because with the day still on the Studio date schedule a parent calling 9:15 to 11:15 would otherwise ring the phone over the 911 call and interrupt the dispatcher, then taps the Emergency call button on the lock screen and dials 911 with no passcode; the hardened Do Not Disturb sends every incoming call to the camp's voicemail greeting while leaving outgoing calls untouched; when the 911 call ends, the oldest camper turns Do Not Disturb off by the same lock-screen path, no passcode needed, because 911 dispatchers call back the reporting number, and the hardened Do Not Disturb that kept the line clear during the call would send that callback to the camp's voicemail greeting unheard: the callback rings through and the oldest camper answers the dispatcher first, and a parent call ringing in the same minute goes unanswered while the dispatcher is on the line, because the parent message the Tier 3 order holds is armed at the T-6w build, not queued mid-crisis: a pre-written "instructor incapacitated, 911 called, group held" broadcast to every session parent, the founder's cell, and the designated break-glass adult's cell, scheduled in the Twilio console for a fixed send at 11:16 that teaching day, fires only if the instructor never cancels it, and no camper touches the phone to send it, because the phone is locked and nobody knows the passcode; the founder's cell and the break-glass adult's cell are on the recipient list so the W-2 instructor's employer and the keyholder learn the same minute parents do, which is the trigger for the owner-login fallback named in the sick-day bullet below; the second oldest's role is the aloud buddy count, keeping the group inside the instructor's sightline while the oldest camper runs the phone; then the oldest camper reads the venue address off the reverse of the pickup-authorization list, and if the dispatcher asks what is wrong with the instructor, reads the instructor medical card clipped face-down beside the phone on the instructor table (the card written in the pilot prep week and re-verified at the T-6w build: conditions, allergies, current meds, emergency contact), read to the dispatcher only, never aloud to the room, nobody queues a parent message mid-crisis, because nobody can reach the Twilio console from the locked phone; the pre-scheduled broadcast fires at 11:16 on its own if the instructor never cancels it, so the parent comms still arrive after the emergency response, the order the Tier 3 chain holds, and nobody touches the med lockbox, the tools, or any medication. The script is walked at the parent walkthrough night so every parent knows exactly what their child has been told to do.
  • Instructor incapacitation on a field walk: the route-grounded variantThe room script names the instructor table, the lockable door, and the venue address off the pickup-list reverse, and none of those exist on a field walk: the instructor is down on the sidewalk, the phone is in the field bag, and the venue address is the wrong address. The field variant is rehearsed once at the first field walk-out, the Day 2 safari count, in the two minutes the count already takes, the walk-out count names the card in the zip phone pocket, with the same two oldest campers taking the named roles, and it is walked at the parent walkthrough night beside the room script, so the promise made to parents names both. The freeze call is the oldest camper saying "Freeze, buddies" once: the group stops, buddy pairs hold, everyone sits on the grass edge or the sidewalk, never in the road, and nobody leaves the route. The oldest camper pulls the instructor's phone out of the field bag's named zip phone pocket, a pocket set at Session Zero Hour 4 separate from the front pocket that holds the abort cards, and the script names the locked-phone path because the phone is locked and the oldest camper does not know the passcode: the oldest camper opens Control Center on an iPhone, or the quick-settings shade on an Android, from the lock screen with no passcode, taps Do Not Disturb on, the T-6w-hardened Do Not Disturb that a redial cannot pierce (repeated calls off, no Emergency Bypass contacts), because with the day still on the Studio date schedule a parent calling 9:15 to 11:15 would otherwise ring the phone over the 911 call, then taps the Emergency call button on the lock screen and dials 911 with no passcode; when the 911 call ends, the oldest camper turns Do Not Disturb off by the same lock-screen path, no passcode needed, so the dispatcher's callback rings through, and answers the dispatcher first, a parent call ringing in the same minute going unanswered while the dispatcher is on the line, because the parent message the Tier 3 order holds is armed at the T-6w build, not queued mid-crisis: the same pre-written broadcast, fixed send at 11:16 that teaching day, fires only if the instructor never cancels it, and nobody queues anything from the locked phone; if the instructor can speak, the instructor talks to dispatch, and if the instructor cannot, the oldest camper reads the route card's printed 911 line: the route street, the nearest cross street, and the mapped hard shelter, set when the route was mapped at the T-2 site visit, because the pickup-list reverse names the venue and the group is not at the venue. On the Day 2 around-the-block safari the group is venue-adjacent, so the script falls back to the venue address off the pickup-list reverse; on a mapped-route walk it reads the route card; if the dispatcher asks what is wrong with the instructor, the oldest camper reads the instructor medical card from the zip phone pocket (the card written in the pilot prep week and re-verified at the T-6w build: conditions, allergies, current meds, emergency contact), read to the dispatcher only, never aloud to the group. The second oldest runs the aloud buddy count, keeps the group inside the instructor's sightline, and nobody touches the med lockbox, the tools, the field kits, or the epinephrine, exactly like the room script. The Twilio parent message is never queued mid-crisis: it is pre-scheduled at the T-6w build and fires at 11:16 only if the instructor never cancels it, so the parent comms still arrive after the emergency response, the same order the room script and the Tier 3 chain hold. Every field incident logs to the Airtable incident log when the group is back at the venue, and the sick-day bullet's mid-lesson trigger still applies: if the day cannot finish, the day counts as cancelled for refund purposes. No budget-line changes: paper inside the $150 snacks-and-prints operating line; no timing changes, the rehearsal rides the walk-out count.
  • Lost camper on a field walk: the field variantThe room lost-camper drill (freeze, count, the venue searched inward from the last known position, Tier 3 at five minutes) assumes four walls and a spare pair of hands: on a field walk there is no venue to search and one adult cannot leave eleven campers to search for one. Detection is the count-out/count-back ritual the field-missions bullet already runs at every transition: the gap is found at a count, or the moment the instructor sees it in the sightline. The instructor calls "Freeze, buddies" once, and the group stops in buddy pairs, sits at the grass edge or on the sidewalk, off the road, inside the instructor's sightline, nobody leaving the route. The instructor re-counts aloud and the missing camper's buddy names what they last saw: where, when. If the missing camper is visible in the instructor's line of sight, the group walks as one, single file, together to close the gap, then reforms buddy pairs and re-counts aloud; nobody runs ahead and the single-group rule never breaks. If the missing camper is not visible, it is Tier 3 immediately, the same order the runaway line holds: 911 first, from the instructor's phone in the field bag's named zip phone pocket, the location read off the route card's printed 911 line (route street, nearest cross street, mapped hard shelter) on a mapped-route walk, or the venue address off the pickup-list reverse on the Day 2 around-the-block safari, the same fallback the incapacitation variant uses; the missing camper's name comes off the pickup-authorization list in the instructor folder, which rides the field bag, and the clothing description comes from the buddy, the accountability witness. Nobody searches: no one chases a runaway while 11 wait holds on the walk exactly as in the room. The group holds together at the last-seen point on the mapped route, off the road, where the instructor can see the route both ways, and moves only as one group, and only on the dispatcher's direction, to the nearest T-2 hard shelter or back to the venue. The Twilio parent call goes out right after the 911 call, the Tier 3 order, unchanged. Every field incident logs to the Airtable incident log with a timestamp when the group is back at the venue. The field variant is rehearsed once at the Day 3 walk-out count in the two minutes the count already takes (Day 2's count carries the incapacitation rehearsal), and it is walked at the parent walkthrough night beside the room script and the incapacitation variant, so the promise made to parents names all three. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints; no timing changes, the rehearsal rides the count the instructor already staffs.
  • Restroom at the venue: one at a time, the door stays in sightThe restroom procedure is venue-scoped: one camper at a time, in the single-use restroom beside the main room whose door is visible from the instructor's seat (the §07 venue spec item). The instructor never leaves the room and line of sight never breaks: the camper calls the run out loud, the other eleven stay in the room with the instructor, and tool stations go hands-off while a camper is out of the room, hands back on when they return. No pairs, no lines at the door, no hallway trips: the rule holds through the 60-minute lesson and the 45-minute project block alike. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints.
  • Restroom on a field walk: the walk aborts, the venue restroom waitsThe restroom procedure above is venue-scoped (the single-use restroom beside the main room, one at a time, door visible from the instructor's seat), and the field-missions bullet's mapped routes carry no public restrooms and no entering buildings, so a camper needing a restroom on a walk has one honest answer under the one-adult model: the walk comes home. Prevention rides the walk-out count: the instructor names the restroom gate in the route brief every walk-out morning ("restroom now, because the walk has no restroom"); campers who need it go one at a time at the venue while the rest of the group stays seated in the U hearing the route brief, and the instructor never leaves the room, so the single-use door stays visible from the instructor's seat exactly as the venue procedure requires. If a camper needs the restroom mid-walk, the camper says so and the walk aborts early on the same form the lightning bullet uses: aloud count, the group walks straight back to the venue together along the mapped route, no splits, no buddy pair peeling off to find a restroom, no ducking into a shop. On return the camper uses the venue restroom while the group does the walk-back count; the mission continues on the indoor alternate or the remaining walk time becomes self-directed project work on the day's handout, which the route brief already names. The no-shame rule is stated matter-of-factly at every walk-out, so asking is the expected action and no camper is ever asked to wait it out. No budget-line changes: no new materials, no new prints; no timing changes, the gate rides the two minutes the walk-out count already takes.
  • Behavior and conflict, one adult, no side roomTwelve campers ages 10 to 14 and one adult, so disputes are handled in the open, never in a side room: there is no office, no pull-aside, no camper leaving the room with the instructor alone, and the line-of-sight model holds during a dispute exactly as during an incident. Day 1's 0:00-0:10 open sets the room contract in its three-minute camp-rules segment: the instructor reads three rules aloud, campers repeat each back, and the rules go on the truth-rubric poster board for all ten days: (1) voices down and tools down when a person is speaking; (2) hands off other campers' work; (3) when stuck, ask the handout before you ask the room. A dispute is settled at the table, in the open, with the instructor stating facts only ("the two sets of parts go back to their owners' tables now"), no feelings, no verdict on who started it. A camper who cannot keep participating takes the reset chair beside the instructor's table, inside the room and in line of sight, with a blank journal page and five minutes; the group keeps working. If the camper is still out after five minutes, the parent gets a Twilio heads-up that hour, and the camper's buddy keeps the buddy station warm. Physical aggression (a shove, a strike, a thrown object) is a behavioral Tier 2: the group freezes as in Incident handling, tools down, the instructor counts twelve, the parent is called for same-hour pickup, and the camper does not return until the instructor and the parent agree the room contract holds again. A second same-session incident ends the session for that camper with a pro-rated refund under the published refund policy. Every behavioral incident is logged in the Airtable incident log with a timestamp, the same as any Tier 1 or above.
  • Instructor sick day: one adult, zero backupThe staffing model has exactly one background-checked adult: no assistant, no CIT, no substitute roster. A day without the instructor is a day the camp cannot run, because the room, the field, and the project block all require the instructor's presence every minute. The instructor does not teach sick. If the instructor wakes up too ill to hold the line-of-sight count (fever, vomiting, anything that degrades supervision), the day cancels: a Twilio cancellation goes to every parent by 8 AM, the same 8 AM call the weather pivot uses; the same 8 AM moment pulls that day's date off the Studio flow's date schedule in the Twilio console, a one-minute edit from the instructor's phone with no venue needed, so a parent who misses the cancellation text and calls between 9:15 and 11:15 lands on the camp's voicemail greeting instead of ringing the sick instructor's phone, and the removed date stays off the schedule so the ring rule always matches the days the session actually runs; the same 8 AM Twilio console moment also cancels that day's pre-scheduled crisis broadcast, 11:16 that day or 11:46 on the Day 10 showcase, about one more minute with the console already open, because the 11:15 pickup-row cancel never runs on a day with no pickup: without this step the dead-man's switch would fire a false "instructor incapacitated, 911 called, group held at the venue, pickup instructions to follow" text at 11:16 on top of the 8 AM cancellation, the exact contradictory panic the broadcast exists to prevent. This broadcast cancel runs only on the 8 AM pre-lesson path: a mid-lesson incapacitation leaves the broadcast armed, because that is the day it is needed and the instructor never reaches the console mid-crisis; the enrollment confirmation states this policy so no parent is surprised. The cancelled day refunds pro-rated through Stripe at one-tenth of tuition ($42.50 per seat, processed within 48 hours) on the tuition collection and refunds API the launch plan already carries; the session resumes the next scheduled day with no make-up added. Two consecutive cancelled days, or three in one session, trigger the hard-cancel terms: a full refund of the remaining days via Stripe and the wind-down checklist, the same gate the launch plan uses at T-4. The wind-down checklist is seven steps, run by the instructor from the phone except where a step names the founder, in this order: (1) in the Twilio console, cancel every remaining pre-scheduled crisis broadcast for the session's future teaching dates, 11:16 each day or 11:46 on the Day 10 showcase, because the 11:15 pickup-row cancel and the 8 AM sick-day cancel only ever touch the current day, and without this step the dead-man's switch would fire a false "instructor incapacitated, 911 called, group held at the venue, pickup instructions to follow" text at 11:16 on a date the session no longer runs; (2) in the same console moment, pull every remaining teaching date off the Studio flow's date schedule, so an in-hours parent call lands on the camp's Twilio voicemail instead of ringing the instructor's phone; (3) send the hard-cancel notice to every session parent over the camp number and process the remaining-day full refunds via Stripe within 48 hours; (4) notify the venue in writing that the remaining dates release, so the hold and the deposit terms close out cleanly; (5) mark the session cancelled in Airtable, so no waitlist ping, roster export, or Session Zero print batch treats it as live; (6) in the same Twilio console moment as steps 1 and 2, edit the Say widget's TTS text in the Studio flow to the cancelled-session line, the same mechanism the T-6w build uses for the hours line (one line of text in the console, never a recording), because step 2 routes post-cancel parent calls to that greeting and the live greeting still says "camp hours are 9:15 to 11:15, leave a message, read first thing each teaching morning," which is false after a hard-cancel: the cancelled line reads, "You have reached the camp number. This session is cancelled; there are no further teaching mornings. The hard-cancel notice sent over this number carries the details and the refund timing."; (7) once steps 1 through 6 are confirmed done, the founder revokes the instructor's restricted Twilio user from the owner login, so no provisioned login survives a cancelled session: the T-4 hard gate and the binding re-score gate fire after the T-10w hire pinned the provisioning to the background-check-clear day, and the sick-day and mid-lesson hard-cancels fire after the instructor has been running console moments on that login all session, so every path that runs this checklist has a live instructor login that no future step will use again; the revocation is founder labor on the owner login, never instructor labor, because a login cannot retire itself mid-checklist and the incapacitation path runs this checklist when the instructor may be unable; when the founder teaches, no instructor user was ever provisioned, so step 7 is a one-line check that no restricted user exists, not a revocation; and when this checklist runs, the session never reaches the venue section's move-out, so the move-out revocation never fires and the two revocations cannot double-revoke. A hard-cancel triggered off a mid-lesson incapacitation runs the same checklist as soon as the instructor is able, the same scoping the date-schedule pull-off already carries for that path. If the instructor never becomes able (hospitalized, unconscious, unreachable), the founder runs steps 1 through 6 from the owner login: if the instructor has not confirmed by 4 PM that teaching day that they can run the checklist, the founder assumes they cannot and runs it that evening, unless the founder holds a logged false-fire account for that day's 11:16 broadcast under the Day 3 false-fired stand-down rule, in which case the run is stood down and the founder sends the parent correction text instead; the run happens on the owner login the founder holds, because the dead-man's broadcast fired to the founder's cell at 11:16 and every step is founder-reachable, steps 1, 2, and 6 in the Twilio console, step 3's Stripe refunds and step 5's Airtable cancellation on the founder's operator accounts, step 4's venue release as the founder's written notice; step 7 is unchanged, already founder labor on the owner login once steps 1 through 6 are confirmed done. Behind the founder's 4 PM run sits the one deeper trigger, for the evening the founder themself cannot be reached: if the 11:16 broadcast fired and by 9 PM that teaching day neither the instructor nor the founder has confirmed that the checklist ran or will run that evening, and two contact attempts to the founder, a call and a text, both fail, the designated break-glass adult opens the sealed envelope and runs steps 1 through 7 from the owner login that evening, the same owner-login labor the founder would have done: steps 1, 2, and 6 in the Twilio console, step 3's refunds and step 5's Airtable cancellation on the envelope's Stripe and Airtable logins, step 4's venue release sent as the camp's written notice under the venue agreement's break-glass cancellation-notice clause, invoked through the keyholder roster authority, addressed to the roster's re-confirmed contact, or to all three roster lines, the last-known contact, the signing counterparty, and the agreement's legal-notice address, when the roster marks the T-2w re-confirm unconfirmed, the notice sent by email under the clause's email sufficiency, carrying the clause's sender-identity pin, so the venue verifies the sender by the quoted roster name rather than the personal sending domain, and the clause's subject-line convention, so the release names the clause and the remaining session dates in the subject line rather than sitting under a generic subject, with the remaining dates read off the printed session calendar sealed in the envelope, the keyholder circling the dates printed after the broadcast night, copying them onto the subject-line template's blank date line, and sending the release with the template's clause-named subject line verbatim, and the notice body quoted off the envelope's clause-quote card and roster card, the keyholder copying the boxed operative passage out of the card's isolated box and the roster entry into the email body verbatim, so the convention is executed by copying from the box, not by calendar arithmetic, memory typing, fragment-picking, or starting from an adjacent unmarked line at 9 PM, and effective upon sending by the clause's timing so an unreachable venue cannot later claim it was late, and step 7's revocation as owner-login labor, because the envelope holds the owner login itself. The envelope is never opened on a day the broadcast did not fire, and never before the 9 PM check, so a reachable founder or an able instructor never loses the evening to a false break-glass. A logged false-fire account for that day's 11:16 broadcast under the Day 3 false-fired stand-down rule counts as the founder's confirmation for the 9 PM check, so the envelope is never opened on a stood-down false fire either. In a founder-operated session the founder is the incapacitated instructor, so the founder's 4 PM run cannot happen; the same 9 PM break-glass trigger applies instead, and the keyholder runs the checklist that evening from the envelope, with the login rotation riding the next console moment the founder can reach. The parent-facing pieces are covered in the meantime, because the 11:16 broadcast told every parent the instructor is incapacitated and 911 was called, and the hard-cancel notice rides the keyholder's step 3 run that evening. If the instructor falls ill mid-lesson, the rehearsed incapacitation script runs (the Day 1 walk) and the day counts as cancelled for refund purposes. Nobody can reach the Twilio console mid-crisis, so the date stays on the Studio date schedule, and the script's Do Not Disturb step is the mid-session analogue of the 8 AM pull-off: it keeps any parent call from ringing the incapacitated instructor's phone while the oldest camper runs the 911 call from it, every incoming call rolling to the camp's voicemail greeting, and the Do Not Disturb turns off when the 911 call ends, because that is when the dispatcher's callback may ring through; the date comes off the Studio date schedule as soon as the instructor is able, which is when the cancelled-day refund formalizes. No budget-line changes: this is a contingency, not a line item; the Stripe refund machinery already exists in the launch plan.
  • Tech outage: no Wi-Fi, no power, dead printerEvery lesson leans on the venue: Wi-Fi (the model tabs, the Day 2 shared album, the Airtable med, incident, and attendance logs, the QR check-in at the door table), power (the 8 cart laptops, the venue printer, the 3D printers), the venue printer (the Day 2 contact-sheets, the Day 5 photo pages, the Wednesday restock reprints), and the room screen (the fail-of-the-day beats, the teardown video). The page named no procedure for any of them dying mid-day, a real gap under the one-adult model: the instructor cannot spend twenty minutes on a router while twelve campers wait. The rule is five minutes: the instructor may reboot, rejoin, or re-plug, from inside the room only, line of sight never broken. After five minutes, or immediately when the power goes (there is nothing to troubleshoot), the day flips to its paper version and the lost segment is not chased. Every dependent segment has a named analog cut-over, all on paper the page already prints: the room-screen beats run from the AI Fail binders at each team table; the model-tab grades run from the answer keys in the instructor folder, which every handout already promises; the shared-album upload runs from the cameras' playback screens with the paper tally sheets carrying the record; the contact-sheets are a convenience, not the lesson, so the labeling runs on the tally sheets when the printer dies. The Airtable logs and the QR check-in degrade to the paper backstop filed in the instructor folder at Session Zero Hour 4 beside the answer keys: the printed pickup-authorization list, a one-page paper attendance sheet, and a one-page paper med/incident log sheet, two new pages on the Hour 1 print run, folded into the $150 snacks-and-prints operating line. Outage entries are written there during the day and transcribed to Airtable the same evening, never left unlogged. The 8 AM Twilio calls (the weather pivot, the sick-day cancellation) run on the instructor's cellular phone, not venue Wi-Fi, so an internet outage never blocks the 8 AM call. No budget-line changes: this is procedure, plus two sheets of paper.
  • Qualifications and checksThe instructor is background-checked (Checkr) and first-aid/CPR certified before any session is announced, AB 506 trained (California's youth-service training), and insured under the camp's $600/session general liability policy with workers' comp bound when the instructor is hired W-2. The instructor also serves as the on-site Health Supervisor the county organized-camp permit requires: first-aid/CPR certified, holder of the med lockbox and the medication cooler-bag combination, owner of the med log and the incident log. One adult, two named responsibilities. The incident log, the med log, and the attendance record are camp property, exported to the parent at session end, and kept on file for three years. If the background check is not clean by T-10, the session postpones: that is kill gate 3 in the launch plan, and it is not negotiable.

The pilot: where this paper plan meets real kids.

No website can score what only real campers can prove. So the plan names the proof it will collect before any paid session runs: one pilot day, one parent night, measured results, and a published re-score.

  • Pilot day: Saturday, April 17, 2027Eight volunteer campers, four ages 10 to 12 and four ages 13 to 14, recruited through the school scholarship partners, each with a signed parent consent and pickup authorization on file. The four-year spread is deliberate: the 13-14s are the only ones who can answer whether the senior extensions are hard enough, and a pilot with no teenagers in it cannot test them. The day does not try to fit two full sessions into one morning; it tests each risk once, compressed: 8:00-8:30 setup of the pre-kitted pilot paper plus check-in, 8:30-9:15 the Day 1 blind-taste equivalent (45 minutes: 10-minute open with one AI Fail, 25-minute gauntlet with two teams of four and one tray of 20 cards per team, 10-minute reveal and mini truth-rubric on the truth-rubric poster board; laminated checklists issued at the close), 9:15-10:00 the "Failure Autopsy" project block at full 45 minutes, 10:00-10:10 reset, 10:10-10:55 the Day 8 taste-panel equivalent (45 minutes: 5-minute brief, 20-minute panel of the eight best morning pieces at 2.5 minutes each with the guest judge watching, 5-minute judge's gambit, 10-minute QC-gate draft, 5-minute stress test of two drafts), 10:55-11:40 the "QC Gate" project block at full 45 minutes, and 11:40-12:20 the eight five-minute exit interviews, each camper with their own parent, in the room, the other families in the gallery corner in the instructor's sightline, all eight counted until the last authorized-adult dismissal at 12:20. The lesson equivalents are compressed so both lessons and both blocks fit one Saturday; the project blocks run at full length because the block is what the panel is scoring. The Day 8 equivalent's guest judge is one pre-briefed volunteer from the school scholarship partner's faculty, briefed by email the week before, in the room only for the 10:10-10:55 panel block; no fee. The judge is thanked at the room door and shown out at 10:55, the instructor staying in the room with the eight campers, because the safety plan's line-of-sight rule has no exceptions. The pilot instructor is the same person who will teach the summer sessions. The pilot has a cost the page never owned until now: it needs a room, Saturday 8:00 to 12:20 (the schedule above, 4.33 hours), plus the Tuesday parent-walkthrough 45 minutes, all at the session's $75/hr venue rate (about 5.1 hours, about $381); its own April paper: 16 handouts (eight "Failure Autopsy," eight "The QC Gate"), the taste-lesson paper (2 artifact trays of 20 cards, 8 answer cards, 8 laminated Producer's Checklists), 2 1-inch AI Fail binders (the pilot's 2, reusable shop stock, bought once on the $1,500 shop-tools startup line alongside the third binder the June session's third team table needs, not paid from the pilot's $435 all-in total; the 24 blunder sheets print in the April run from the pilot-prep-week selection file, see Phase 6, and slide in for the pilot, refreshed again in June), 8 panel scorecards, 8 QC-gate page templates, and 2 blank poster boards for the Day 1 and Day 8 walls, plus 8 camp journals, one per volunteer, all from an April copy-shop run of about 112 sheets plus the boards plus the journals, markers from shop stock (the June Session Zero print run is two months after the pilot and cannot print them), paid from the pilot's $54 materials slice (the $30 paper plus the $24 journals), inside the $435 all-in total itemized at the end of this paragraph. The journals are not decoration: the pilot runs the Failure Autopsy block at full 45 minutes, and that handout's needs list starts with the Day 1 journal page; the autopsy table, the three binder adds, and the trade-journals stuck protocol all assume it, and the pilot has no session stock to draw on, so eight notebooks join the April run (about $24). The Day 1 one-take teardown video is recorded before the pilot too, not only before the June session: the pilot's 9:15 block needs the same 0:00-0:10 room-screen teardown, and the same file serves June. Materials are the startup equipment the pilot's two lessons exercise, ordered under the Phase 5 T-8 ordering deadline, not re-bought. The pilot's instructor hours are pre-session labor (founder time or the candidate's paid working interview inside the three Gusto months), outside the session's $2,000 pay line. That prep week also carries the one-time digital authoring the June session reuses, about seven and a half hours across thirty-six tasks: the Gauntlet deck (45 minutes), the ten answer keys (60), the logging program (25), the teardown video (15), the wiring diagram (20), nine paper layouts at 15 each, the credit-roll template (15), the blunder selection (30), the ten late-batch pieces (90), and the fail-of-the-day slide (15). The same prep week writes the instructor medical card the incapacitation scripts name: the founder asks the instructor, the same person who will teach the summer sessions, for the four fields, conditions, allergies, current meds, and emergency contact, and writes them on one card cut from the April paper run, face down from then on, so the card exists before the Tuesday parent walkthrough night that walks the script naming it; when the founder teaches, the founder writes their own card. About $435 all in: about $381 of venue time (5.1 hours at $75), about $30 of April paper (112 copy-shop sheets, 2 poster boards, self-seal pouches for the 8 laminated checklists), and about $24 for the 8 pilot journals; the 2 pilot binders are reusable shop stock on the $1,500 shop-tools startup line, not pilot consumables, so the $435 subtotal stands as itemized. It lives in the launch ledger, not the operating budget: the pilot is pre-launch validation, not session delivery. The pilot day gets its own dead-man's switch, because the ten fixed-send broadcasts the T-6w build arms are all June dates and the camp number is only provisioned at T-10w: during the pilot's prep week the founder arms one pre-written "instructor incapacitated, 911 called, group held" broadcast on the owner login, to the eight pilot families, the founder's cell, and the designated break-glass adult's cell, with a fixed send at 12:26 on Sat Apr 17, a plain scheduled send, not a Studio-routed one, because the Studio date schedule the T-6w build enters holds only the ten June teaching dates. The instructor opens the Twilio console on the instructor laptop at the 12:20 count-down dismissal and cancels the broadcast in that console moment, about one extra minute, on the restricted instructor login the T-10w row provisioned, or on the owner login when the founder teaches. A pilot day that runs normally sends nothing; the broadcast fires only on a pilot day the instructor never reaches the console, exactly the day it is needed, the same 11:16 logic the June session runs on its ten dates. No budget-line changes: the camp number's monthly rent rides the existing $140/session Twilio parent-comms line, and the pilot's extra weeks are pre-launch validation spend like the rest of the pilot's $435.
  • What gets measuredFive numbers: handout completion rate inside each 45-minute block, stuck-protocol trigger count per handout, time-on-task against each block's 45-minute plan, segment-by-segment timing of both lesson equivalents against their condensed run-of-shows, and the instructor's incident and near-miss log. Every camper also does a five-minute exit interview: what confused you, where did you get stuck. The four 13-14s answer a senior-only question as well: whether the hard versions (the senior extensions in each handout) were actually hard enough, and where they broke. No opinion questions about how fun it was; the pilot tests teachability, not entertainment.
  • Success barThe pilot counts as evidence if handout completion clears 80 percent inside each block, median time-on-task clears 35 of the 45 minutes in each block, stuck-protocol escalations stay at three or fewer, both lesson equivalents land inside their 45-minute shells, there are zero tool incidents or ratio breaches, and at least three of the four 13-14s say the senior extensions were genuinely hard, not babyish. A miss on the senior-hardness question counts the same as any other miss: the senior extensions get rewritten, the bar retested, and the re-score waits.
  • Parent walkthrough nightThe Tuesday before the pilot, 6:30 to 7:15 PM, parents walk the full safety plan above with the instructor, ask questions, and review the pickup-authorization list in person. Any plan change a parent asks for gets a written answer within 48 hours. This is the first real parent walkthrough the panel asked for, and it happens before any child arrives.
  • Published re-scoreThe §11 panel's scores update on this page after the pilot, with the measured numbers shown alongside them, by Friday, May 14, 2027, three days before the T-4 kill-gate decision on Monday, May 17. If the panel does not clear 9.0 after the pilot, no summer session is sold: the date, the bar, and the re-score are all public, which is what makes them binding.

05 · Capstone

Every camper leaves with proof they did what the machine couldn't.

The capstone isn't a diorama, it's a small, real production with an AI in the crew and a kid in charge. Teams of four pick one track and ship it at Showcase:

Track A · The Field Guide

Verified local dataset

A human-collected, human-verified dataset of the neighborhood (species, sounds, measurements), with a model graded against it and every miss explained.

Track B · The Object

Fabricated thing

A physical product born from an AI design, rescued by human engineering: original prompt, failed print, fixed revision, load test on video.

Track C · The Story

Human-sourced piece

An oral-history audio piece no model could record, a real interview, edited, fact-checked, and published with a producer's credit roll.

Every capstone ships with the same final artifact: the credit roll, one page naming what the AI did, what the humans did, what failed, and who fixed it. Parents don't just see the project; they see their kid taking responsibility for a machine's work. That page is the diploma.

06 · Staff

One instructor, by design.

The whole program is engineered so a single adult can run it: twelve campers, sixty minutes a day, every material pre-kitted, every post-lesson project block self-directed. There is no assistant, no CIT (counselor-in-training), no second pair of eyes, which is exactly why the format, the tool gating, and the admin automation are built the way they are. The instructor is either the founder operating the camp or one hired educator; either way the AB5 classification question is settled, not dodged.

Age-differentiation model (10–14 range): The four-year spread within the cohort is bridged through tiered challenge variants, not separate tracks. Each day's mission ships with a base version (accessible at 10) and an extension layer (expected at 13–14): e.g., Day 2's photo dataset uses GPS tagging for juniors and adds metadata schema design for seniors; Day 5's CAD redesign uses guided templates for 10-year-olds and freeform modeling for 14-year-olds; Day 7's rover gauntlet adds autonomous waypoint navigation for the older cohort. The labels juniors and seniors mean the same ages everywhere on this page: juniors are ages 10–12 and work the base version, seniors are ages 13–14 and work the extension layer, in every lesson plan, handout margin mark, answer key, and the pilot's senior-only exit question. Rotating crew roles give every camper a turn at the materials captain, Wall of Almost curator, and reset lead jobs, performed inside the group, in the instructor's line of sight, never as supervision; the 13–14s get challenge depth from the senior mission extensions, not exclusive staffing roles. Kids who already code get harder variants of every mission, the rubric scales, the thesis doesn't.

The instructor · 1 adult

A generalist maker-educator

One person: teaching experience plus hands-on maker fluency, background-checked, first-aid certified, AB 506 trained. Must be able to run a sensor build, a CAD session, a taste panel, and an interview drill in the same week. Pay: $2,000 per two-week session (10 instruction hours, a 4-hour Session Zero kitting day, 30 minutes of prep each teaching day (5 hours across the session), plus the 15-minute daily drop-off and check-in window at the door table (2.5 hours across the session), plus present-but-silent supervision through the daily project blocks and pickup, the 30-minute mid-week restock, the 3-hour Friday-afternoon print-tending block after Day 5 (June 18, pulling each finished fixed-revision print off the beds once it reads under 50C and shelving the before-and-after pairs on the museum shelf for the showcase), and the 30-minute Day 10 parents' showcase, and the ~20-minute Day 10 gallery strike after the 11:45 dismissal, roughly 33.3 hours all in, about $60/hr blended). If hired rather than founder-operated, the instructor is a W-2 employee on Gusto payroll with California workers' comp bound before Day 1. Contractor arrangements are off the table: under California's ABC test (AB5, Labor Code §2775) an on-site, on-schedule instructor delivering the camp's core service is an employee, full stop.

Camper crew · rotating, all campers 10–14

Responsibility, not supervision

Three campers per day take crew jobs with a printed checklist: materials captain (kit distribution), Wall of Almost curator, reset lead (end-of-hour reset). The rotation runs through the whole cohort, so every camper serves about every fourth day, and the jobs are built so a 10-year-old can run them straight off the printed sheet: the checklist, not the camper's age, is the qualification, and the instructor re-checks the materials captain's kit count before kits go out. These are leadership roles performed inside the group, in the instructor's line of sight at all times. Nobody under 18 ever supervises, and no camper is ever out of the group.
