Enrollment sets no age-mix floor, so gating the jobs to 13–14s would leave them unfillable in a younger cohort; the paper-path logic applies here too.

The automation layer

Admin that needs no human

Everything a second adult used to absorb, check-in, parent pings, attendance, pickup authorization records, incident timestamps, is handled by the API stack in §10 (Airtable, Twilio, Typeform). The solo instructor teaches; the software does the paperwork. That is what makes one adult enough.

Prep · Session Zero + 30 min/day

The kitting day: four hours, budgeted, paid

Every day's box is packed by the same person who teaches, in one four-hour Session Zero at the venue the Saturday before the session, not the night before. This is the work the daily 30-minute checklists stand on. It is scheduled, paid, and inside the instructor's hours, not a favor done after hours. After Session Zero, each day's prep is genuinely 30 minutes or less: first the 8:45 morning read of the camp-number inbox, about five minutes, with a "got it" reply to every overnight parent message; then open the day-box, lay out twelve stations, run the tool-safety check, charge the shared devices.

the ten questions live in the §03b printing spec), 60 artifact cards (the numbered Gauntlet deck built on the instructor laptop in the week-before window), 39 mission/claim/verdict cards (12 mission, 15 claim, 12 verdict, counted against the Day 2 and Day 3 materials lists), 12 verdict-report templates (Day 3, one per camper, counted against the Day 3 materials list), 12 Day 1 answer cards, 12 producer checklists (laminated in Hour 2, packed in the Day 1 box), 36 blunder sheets (3 binders x 12, this spring's blunders, selected and laid out on the instructor laptop in the week-before window, see Phase 6), 12 question-craft sheets, 12 transcription cards, 12 wiring-diagram cards, 6 queue slips, 6 blank claim tags, 12 debug log sheets, 12 failure log sheets (Day 5), 12 before-and-after portfolio pages (Day 5), 12 overnight log charts (Day 4), 2 ship lists (one posted, one spare), plus the Day 2 grading sheet ("AI vs. you") for the grading station, 12 deploy log sheets and 12 graph charts (Day 4), 12 tally sheets (Day 2 only), 12 panel scorecards (Day 8), 12 QC-gate page templates (Day 8), 12 credit-roll templates (Day 9), 12 diplomas (Day 10), 12 taste-panel award cards (Day 10), the showcase program master (one page, filled in Thursday from the ship list; Any failed battery gets a labeled bag and a replacement ordered that day, never a hope and a prayer. Join the laptop cart to the venue wi-fi, load the Day 2 image-model tab on all 8 cart laptops (the §07 12-seat school-managed AI license already covers all 8: three teams need the tab during the Day 2 label party while the instructor assembles contact-sheets on the grading-station machine, so a single-machine tab would queue twelve campers) and the Day 6 chatbot tab on all 8 cart laptops (the Day 6 materials promise the tab on laptops, plural, and its prep opens it on laptops, plural: six pairs testing questions inside the 25-minute question-craft block cannot queue on one machine; the 12-seat school-managed AI license in §07 covers all 8), copy the 20-thumbnail contact-sheet template file, built on the instructor laptop in the week-before window, onto the one labeled grading-station machine (the template the Tuesday label party assembles the 12 contact-sheets from; the template lives only there, so the Tuesday pull and return are unambiguous), and confirm the content filter holds on the cart; create the camp's photos account on the instructor laptop, a camp-owned account the two shared albums below are created on, signed in on that machine, writing the account's username and password into the launch plan's keyholder roster at creation, because the Day 2 prep's grading-station sign-in runs on a different machine, the labeled grading-station laptop off the cart, and no step has ever recorded the credentials; because the Day 2 and Day 6 materials, the Day 2 prep's grading-station sign-in, and the two album-creation steps all name "the camp photos account" and no step has ever created it; create the "Day 2 Data Safari" shared album on the camp photos account (link-only sharing), bookmark the album link on all 8 cart laptops, and time one 100-photo test upload from a USB SD card reader on the venue Wi-Fi: three cards in parallel need to clear in about five minutes for the Tuesday 10:10-10:15 upload beat to hold. A venue that fails the timing test gets the same treatment as a network failure, the venue's problem to fix by Monday, not a teaching-morning surprise. Create the "Day 6 Showcase Interviews" shared album on the camp photos account (link-only sharing), bookmark the album link on the instructor laptop, and time one test audio upload from a recorder over USB: one 8-minute file needs to land in about a minute for the Monday block-end upload beat to hold. Stage 1 USB cable matching the recorders' transfer port on the equipment shelf as reusable shop stock. Load the laptop files from the instructor laptop: copy the logging-program source to each of the 8 cart laptops (the Day 4 materials promise it pre-loaded on laptops) and to the Day 4 USB stick, pulled from the Day 4 box; copy the 12 junior CAD template files to each of the 8 cart laptops (the Day 5 materials name them and the Friday prep opens them on laptops); copy the finished pre-fixed CAD template file (built on the instructor laptop in the week-before window) to the Day 5 USB stick, pulled from the Day 5 box. Open each loaded copy once from a camper laptop to confirm it reads, then re-pack both sticks in their boxes before the Hour 4 lock. A file that was never loaded and never opened is a wish, not a plan. Then confirm the instructor laptop's own pre-session file load, the machine the Day 1 and Day 6 materials lines call pre-loaded: open the Day 1 fail-of-the-day slide, the Day 6 fail-of-the-day slide, the one-take teardown video file, the print-ready Day 4 forecast sheet, and the print-ready Day 7 route prediction (the teardown was recorded before the session and both prediction sheets were generated in the week-before window, so Hour 3 verifies, not authors). If any of the five is missing, its absence is caught on the kitting Saturday, not on a teaching morning.
  • Hour 4: the safety and admin pass. Test the soldering iron and the fume extractor filter, stage one fresh solder-wire spool and the spare extractor filter in the Day 4 box (replacing any spare a prior session's Day 4 prep consumed, the Day 5 test-rig spares' same rule, off the Day 4 consumables row's extractor filter share), stock the Day 4 burn kit (burn gel packets, burn dressings, instant cold packs) and stage it in the Day 4 box beside the station, and stage the 2 spare USB cables on the equipment shelf with the labeled charging tray (replacing any spare a prior session's Day 4 prep consumed, off the Day 4 consumables row's USB cable spares), then lock the tested station, cord wrapped, in the Day 4 box before the closet lock. Set the labeled combination-lock med lockbox's combination at the equipment shelf, a code distinct from the door-table lockbox and the cooler bag, held by the instructor only, and stage the med lockbox there (it sits on the instructor table during lessons and rides in the instructor's field bag on field missions). Check the camp first-aid kit against the med/allergy sheet, restock it, and stage it on the equipment shelf beside the med lockbox; assemble the sick kit (a digital thermometer with probe covers, disposable gloves, absorbent pads, emesis bags, disinfectant wipes, and zip bags; about $35 of reusable stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line) and stage it with the first-aid kit on the equipment shelf, so the safety plan's camper-illness script has its tray from day one; the kit sits on the instructor table during lessons and rides inside the camp first-aid kit's bag on field missions, so the exact field-bag manifest does not change; set the cooler bag's combination at the equipment shelf, a code distinct from the door-table lockbox and the med lockbox, held by the instructor only, and stage the lockable medication cooler bag with one frozen ice pack inside (both packs frozen at home the night before the kitting Saturday, the second starting in the instructor's home freezer), next to the med lockbox, and stick the thermometer strip on the cooler bag's outside panel. Stage the two 24-bottle water cases beside the field bag on the equipment shelf; on field-mission mornings one full case goes in the bag's side sleeve before the count-out. Stage the epinephrine trainer pen (one reusable unit, bought once in the startup shop-tools line) with the med lockbox, so the Session Zero safety rehearsal runs on the actual device; it then rides in the field bag for the session. Clip the laminated allergy action plans for every camper with a food allergy or EpiPen authorization on file to the instructor's field bag's outside panel, next to the EpiPens, per the safety plan's Medications list, so the morning count's field-bag list and the safety plan name the same clipped set. Print the pickup-authorization list with the camp number (provisioned at T-10w on the Twilio lines) printed on it, with a footer line naming camp hours and the morning read ("camp hours 9:15 to 11:15; messages are read first thing each teaching morning"), so a parent changing pickup at the door can message it on the spot and knows when the message will be read; print the venue's street address on the list's reverse, the exact address the 911 dispatcher hears in the instructor-incapacitation drill. send one live test text to the camp number from the door table and confirm it lands on the instructor's phone. Then open the phone's Settings and confirm automatic date-and-time is toggled on: the close-of-day re-verification reads this phone's automatic date, so the toggle is the authority's on-switch; if it is off, turn it on and leave it on all session, and if the phone sat on a manual date after travel, let one network-synced date read land before relying on it. Clip the instructor medical card face-down beside the instructor phone on the instructor table, so the room incapacitation script's clip line has its staged artifact; it rides in the field bag's zip phone pocket on field missions, per the safety plan's field-mission morning motion. Initial the check on the Session Zero checklist beside the test-text confirmation. If the instructor phone is replaced or factory-reset mid-session, the new or reset phone gets the toggle check before its first close-of-day re-verification: open the phone's Settings, confirm automatic date-and-time is toggled on, turn it on and leave it on all session if it is off, let one network-synced date read land, and initial the re-check in the morning checklist margin; the close-of-day re-verification never reads a new or reset phone's date until the re-check is initialed, so a replaced or wiped device cannot hand the re-verification an unverified date. If the replacement phone cannot reach a network before its first close-of-day re-verification, because its line is not activated and it has no data and no venue Wi-Fi, the re-check does not stall the close: on a named no-data branch the instructor logs 'replacement phone has no network, re-check not completed, date unverified ' with the date in the morning checklist margin, and the close-of-day re-verification runs the dead-phone close fallback on this live phone, the margin-line read alone with a 'phone has no network, date not cross-checked' margin log and a reach-a-network-before-next-prep clause, so the unverified date is still never read. The re-check gives the network-synced date read a fixed wait budget of five minutes of wall time at the close, timed on the room clock, and inside the budget it pins an attempt sequence of three named attempts, so no passive wait can burn the budget on one stalled attempt: attempt one is the initial read with automatic date-and-time already confirmed on, watching the read for the first two minutes; attempt two fires at the start of the third minute, an airplane-mode cycle, airplane mode on for thirty seconds then off, then a fresh read; attempt three is the venue network join, venue Wi-Fi or the founder's hotspot, then one final read before the budget ends; and if the fifth minute ends with no synced read the no-data branch fires at once and the re-verification proceeds with the margin-line read alone, so a stalled sync can never hold the close past five minutes. Every attempt also leaves an attempt log in the morning checklist margin beside the re-check: after each attempt the instructor writes the attempt number, the room-clock time, and initials, so a failed attempt always leaves a paper trace, and each failed attempt's line also carries one failure word, its failure signature: stale when the phone displayed an old unmoved date, spinning when the sync indicator ran without resolving, blank when no date display appeared at all, so the margin records why the sync failed and not just that it did; when the margin's words disagree, stale on one attempt and spinning on another, the mixed-margin rule names which word outranks the others: the latest attempt's word outranks the earlier ones, because it reflects the phone's most recent state, so the morning retry chases the latest attempt's fix first and only falls back to the earlier words if the latest fix lands and the read still fails; the fallback-chain cap ends the chase at two morning retries: the first morning's retry runs the latest attempt's fix, the second morning's retry runs the fallback fix from the earlier words, and if the second morning's retry still fails after its fix lands, the instructor stops chasing the margin's words and takes the dead-phone close fallback, the margin-line read alone with the margin log standing, at that close and every later close, instead of a third morning retry; only a genuinely new network, an activated line, venue Wi-Fi, or a founder hotspot not available on any earlier attempt, restarts the chase as a new first attempt. When the cap fires, the instructor writes the cap log line in the morning checklist margin beside that close's margin log and initials it beside the words: 'chain capped after second morning retry, fallback read used, date not cross-checked ', so the capped stop carries the same attribution as the successful-read log, whose read log carries the attempt number, the synced date, the room-clock time, and initials. The ordinary no-network close keeps the standing margin log 'phone has no network, date not cross-checked', so the margin shows a capped chain and an ordinary no-network close as two different lines. The date slot on the cap log line always carries the close's calendar date, never the phone's displayed date, so the paper trail never reads the unverified phone date, the one the line says was not cross-checked, as a fact. The date slot takes the spelled-out month and day, written 'October 2', never digits like '10/2', so the paper trail never reads an ambiguous date as a fact. A successful read logs the attempt number, the synced date, the room-clock time, and initials, and the margin ends up showing the full sequence of attempts the close actually ran. The synced-date slot on the successful-read log takes the spelled-out month and day, written 'October 2', never digits like '10/2', so the paper trail never reads an ambiguous date as a fact. The no-data branch fires when the budget ends without a synced read, never on the phone's signal display, so a phone showing bars with no data takes the same branch. At the next morning's 8:45 prep the instructor reads the failure words before the retry, because a run of spinning lines points the retry at the network while a run of stale lines points it at the phone's date storage, then retries the toggle check on the replacement phone when a network is available, an activated line, venue Wi-Fi, or a hotspot from the founder's cell; if the phone still has no network the branch re-fires at that close and the margin log stands for a second consecutive day. Assemble the instructor folder (a labeled two-pocket folder, reusable shop stock, the named home the answer keys and the briefing paper have promised since the handout spec): the printed pickup-authorization list and the med/allergy sheet print here; the ten answer keys, the one-page Gauntlet answer key, and the three briefing pages come off the Hour 1 run (the shop-owner sheet's second copy, the Day 6 guest brief, and the Day 8 judge brief: the first shop-owner copy left with the owner in the week-before window, so the folder files three, not four); the one-page accommodation agreement for any camper named at the T-1 call files here beside the med/allergy sheet, so the supports the safety plan promises are on the instructor's paper, not in memory; the Day 6 guest's signed DocuSign media release and the Day 3 pre-verification sheet file in from the week-before window; the one-page paper attendance sheet and the one-page paper med/incident log sheet file in from the Hour 1 run as the tech-outage paper backstop, and the 12 blank incident report forms file in beside the log sheet, and the 10 end-of-day digest templates file in beside the forms, and the two hospital handoff sheets file in beside the med/allergy sheet, so the folder always carries the forms the Incident handling bullet's same-hour rule needs, the digest template the launch plan's duty list promises, and the handoff sheet the Hospital transport bullet's Tier 3 rule needs: the folder sits on the instructor table during lessons and rides in the instructor's field bag on field missions, so the form and the sheet are in the instructor's hand for a Tier 2 or Tier 3 anywhere, venue or field walk. The folder sits on the instructor table during lessons and rides in the instructor's field bag on field missions, so the answer keys, the release, and the pickup-authorization list are always in the instructor's hand, never in a day-box. Stage the Day 5 filament: load one fresh spool in each enclosed printer and set the third spool on the equipment shelf beside the printers as the mid-week spare; slice the two AI-designed phone-stand files (the week-before prompt's two saved design files on the instructor laptop), verify each prints clean, and tape the Tue-Thu print schedule card to each printer, each card naming its printer's design: printer 1 prints design A only, printer 2 prints design B only, two copies per day each, run enclosed in the room during lessons and project blocks. Clear a print shelf on the equipment shelf beside the printers: the instructor pulls each finished phone-stand print off the bed once it reads under 50C and shelves it there, keeping the beds clear for the next run, so the 12 prints accumulate in one named place between Tuesday and the Friday prep that pulls them. The two scraper tools that ship in the printer boxes live on the print shelf from Session Zero on: they are what the 3D-printer safety procedure's cooldown step runs on, and they never travel anywhere else in the session. Stage the Day 5 test rig (500g weight, drop mat, 3 phone dummies) and its 1 spare drop mat and 1 spare phone dummy beside it on the equipment shelf, replacing from the Day 5 test-rig wear share any spare a prior session's Day 5 consumed, the Day 7 stopwatch, the 4 markers, a painter's tape roll (reusable shop stock; the Day 8 QC-gate wall posting needs it, and the tape gun + badge tape lives in the Day 10 box, locked in the storage closet during the silent block), and the Day 7 rover-course kit (2 ramps and 1 pop-up tunnel, reusable shop stock, stored in the equipment room and tagged "Rover course"), and 3 USB SD card readers (reusable shop stock, about $12 for three; the Day 2 upload beat needs all three plugged in on Tuesday morning), the Day 5 queue board (2x3 ft corkboard, reusable shop stock; staged on the equipment shelf at Session Zero and all session, pinned by the door in Day 5 prep, returned at the block's end), and a box of thumbtacks (same staging), and the cutting station kit (2 retractable craft knives, 1 metal ruler, 1 self-healing mat, all reusable shop stock bought once inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line, blades retracted for storage, the same retracted-carry rule the safety plan's cutting procedure runs on) on the equipment shelf. Stage the 12 blunt-tip table scissors in the labeled table-supply caddy on the equipment shelf, bought once as reusable shop stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line; the caddy rides out with the morning room setup and returns to the shelf at teardown, which is what the safety plan's 'sit at the tables freely' line describes during lessons. Stage the fidget tools and the noise-reducing earplugs in the same caddy at the same time, bought once as reusable shop stock inside the $1,500 shop-tools startup line, so the accommodations bullet's sensory supports ride the morning pull the caddy already makes. Set the flatbed document scanner on the door table (it stays there all session, never in a day-box); plug it into the instructor laptop, run one test scan into a per-camper gallery folder, and leave the lid closed. Put the scan-tab sticky-note pad (shop stock) on the door table next to the scanner. Both stay at the door table all session, riding neither a day-box nor the field bag. The lockbox sits on the door table all session and rides neither a day-box nor the field bag. Lock the ten day-boxes in the storage closet; the key lives with the instructor only.

The mid-session restock, 30 minutes, Wednesday evening after Day 3 (June 16): reprint anything the Day 4 and Day 5 boxes came up short on (deploy log sheets, graph charts, failure log sheets, before-and-after portfolio pages), recharge the Day 4 and 5 gear, check the Tue-Thu AI-design print schedule is on track and clear any failed print, re-queueing it from the saved file, and check the filament: if either printer's spool reads under about one-quarter, swap in the mid-week spare from the equipment shelf, since the six fixed revisions print Friday afternoon. Check the venue printer's toner gauge before the restock reprints: if it reads under about one-quarter and the camp's spare cartridge is still sealed in the Day 9 box, swap it in and hand the spent cartridge to venue staff for recycling, and order a fresh spare on the next session's consumables cart. Nothing restocks itself and nothing is bought retail mid-week: the $936/session materials line buys the consumables below as one cart, ordered a month before.

07 · Budget sketch

Honest math for one solo-instructor session.

12 campers × $425 tuition, one two-week session (ten one-hour lessons). Equipment is a one-time startup cost (amortized over 3 summers); the operating table shows a single steady-state session. A donor-funded scholarship bench covers 2 of 12 seats. (The year-1 launch costs, entity, ads, compliance stack, are itemized separately in §10's launch ledger, so these two budgets never double-count.)

08 · Venue & equipment

A makerspace with a yard, two blocks from reality.

The camp needs a room AI has never been in, with a door that opens onto the world it can't see.

Space
  • Makerspace / STEM lab: 1,500+ sq ft with 6 build tables and power drops
  • Separate quiet nook for instructor admin calls and guest arrival, never used for camper sessions (all twelve campers stay in the main room, in the instructor's line of sight)
  • Secure outdoor yard or adjacent park access for data safaris and rover gauntlet, plus the makerspace interior as the mapped indoor alternate for all four outdoor days (the weather pivot lives in the safety plan)
  • Walkable neighborhood (0.25 mi) for Day 3 field verification missions
  • Gallery wall space for the Showcase exhibition
  • Room screen or projector in the main room (the Day 1 teardown video plays on it during the project block; the instructor laptop drives it). The instructor laptop is the instructor's own machine, not a startup-budget line: it arrives with the instructor each morning, drives the room screen, tethers the flatbed scanner, runs the project-block silent admin, and leaves at dismissal, never stored overnight in the venue. The flatbed scanner stays on the door table all session; its cable re-plugs into the laptop each morning
  • Saturday access for the 4-hour Session Zero kitting block (June 12, 8am–12pm): the boxes pack and lock in the venue's storage closet that day; plus a 30-minute Wednesday-evening restock block after Day 3 (June 16); plus a 3-hour Friday-afternoon print-tending block after Day 5 (June 18), when the six fixed-revision files print on the two enclosed printers and the instructor pulls and shelves each finished revision once it reads under 50C; plus the ~20-minute Day 10 gallery strike after the 11:45 dismissal (June 25), when the instructor works the named-home teardown checklist in the §04 supervision table: rover kits to the labeled charging tray, tape gun and sticky pads back in the Day 10 box, markers to the equipment shelf, before-and-after pairs retained or recycled by age on the museum shelf, written-on posters and session paper to recycling; plus a ~1-hour equipment move-out after the Day 10 gallery strike (June 25), when the camp operator packs the ten day-boxes, the laptop cart, the labeled charging tray, the rover-course kit, the print-station gear, the field bag, and the flatbed scanner out of the venue. The move-out is operator labor, done by the camp founder outside the instructor's paid hours (a hired W-2 instructor does no unpaid overtime under California wage law); the founder revokes the instructor's restricted Twilio user at move-out, so no stale login can reach the Studio flow or the scheduled broadcasts between sessions, and it books the venue one more hour, which the §07 venue line bills
  • Lockable on-site storage (closet or room) from the June 12 Session Zero through Day 10 for the ten packed day-boxes, the laptop cart, and the equipment; the instructor holds the only key
  • On-site laser printer (or networked copier) for the 566-sheet Session Zero print run, the Day 2 mid-session contact-sheet print (12 pages), the 3 Thursday-morning AI forecast sheets (one per Day 4 team table, printed in the Day 4 prep), the Day 5 project block's 24 photo pages (printed in the block's first minutes), the Wednesday-evening restock reprints after Day 3 (June 16), and the Day 10 showcase programs (printed Friday morning from the filled-in master, one per camper's party)
  • Reliable venue wi-fi sized for 8 simultaneous laptops plus the instructor laptop, with content filtering the instructor can verify at Session Zero: the Day 2 image-model grading station and the Day 6 chatbot tab both need live internet; the ten project blocks run on paper and keep running either way
  • Single-use restroom adjacent to the main room, its door visible from the instructor's seat: the safety plan's one-at-a-time restroom procedure depends on it, and a multi-stall restroom down the hall would break the one-adult supervision model
Equipment
  • 2× FDM 3D printers (fused deposition modeling, the standard filament-fed desktop type) + ventilation enclosure + filament stock
  • 3× rover robotics kits, shared (one per team of 4)
  • Rover-course kit: 2 ramps, 1 pop-up tunnel, cones, lane tape, packing-peanut swamp (reusable shop stock)
  • 12× micro:bit sensor kits (temp / light / sound, USB rechargeable)
  • 3× field kits: camera, lav mic, recorder, calipers, clipboard, chalk
  • 8-laptop cart with AI tool accounts + content filters
  • Shop basics: hand tools, one soldering station (instructor-run only), the cutting station kit (2 retractable craft knives, 1 metal ruler, 1 self-healing mat), the 12 blunt-tip table scissors, the camp first-aid kit, the instructor's field bag (contents listed at Session Zero Hour 4), the 8-slot NiMH charger (staged on the equipment shelf at Session Zero, charging the Day 7 spare 8-cell set at Hour 3), PPE, the labeled med lockbox, the lockable medication cooler bag with frozen ice pack, a stick-on thermometer strip, fidget tools, and noise-reducing earplugs

09 · Success metrics

We measure the skill, not the vibe.

Camper growth
  • Pre/post assessment: verify-or-trust a set of AI claims, target +40% accuracy on "checked it and here's the evidence"
  • 100% of campers ship a capstone with a completed credit roll
  • Rubric-scored judgment: taste-panel scores align with instructor panel by Day 8
Family verdict
  • Parent NPS (Net Promoter Score, -100 to +100) ≥ 60 at Showcase exit survey
  • ≥ 70% of families say camper "talks about checking AI output at home" (the dinner-table test)
  • ≥ 50% re-enroll or refer for Summer 2028
Program health
  • Enrollment: 12 campers, waitlist ≥ 6 by May 1
  • Safety: zero unsupervised tool incidents; one background-checked adult supervises all 12 campers every minute of every session; field missions move as a single group, no exceptions
  • Financial: sponsorship + scholarship bench fully funded before session 1

10 · Execution plan

The launch plan: automation for the paperwork, humans for the judgment.

This camp can be built by one operator + a stack of real APIs, with humans kept exactly where the law, insurers, and child safety require them: supervising children and owning judgment calls. Below: the full launch plan, seven phases, T-minus timeline, itemized budget, kill gates, and the honest list of steps that cannot be human-free. Every named API was verified to exist in Aug 2026; every marketplace that doesn't expose an API is flagged and given a workaround.

The operating thesis APIs do the paperwork: form the entity, bind insurance, run background checks, push ads, collect tuition, text parents, track rosters. Humans own the judgment: permit walk-throughs, insurance riders, adjudicating a flagged background check, and, non-negotiably, supervising children. Roughly 90% of the launch tasks run without a human touching them; the remaining 10% is the part that makes it legal and safe, compressed into seven single-decision human gates.
Phase 0 · T-22 → T-20 weeks (Jan 2027)

Entity & money

~3 weeks · ~$3,200
  • Stripe Atlas, form the LLC (Delaware via Atlas), EIN auto-issued by the IRS within days, Mercury operating account through Atlas's bank partner. verified API
  • Honest note: Atlas defaults to Delaware; operating in California means foreign qualification ($70) + CA's $800/yr franchise tax anyway. That $870 is real and budgeted below, not a surprise.
  • DocuSign eSignature API, account + templates for the four paper shapes we need: the instructor's W-2 offer and employment agreement (contractor arrangements are off the table; the instructor is a W-2 employee on Gusto payroll), venue agreement, parent waiver, and the Day 6 guest media release (sent the week before the session; the signed copy files into the instructor folder before Session Zero). verified API Pricing honesty: API plans are quote-based; budget $1.8–4k/yr, with a SignWell-class API as a ~$600 fallback if the quote comes in high.
Kill gate 0, Entity + bank not live in 3 weeks → stop. Nothing downstream can hold money or sign paper.
Phase 1 · T-20 → T-16 weeks (Jan–Feb)

Compliance & insurance

~4 weeks · ~$3,450

Regulatory reality first (California, 2026): a seasonal, primarily-recreational summer day camp is exempt from child-care facility licensing (Health and Safety Code §1596.792, with CDSS, the California Department of Social Services, status confirmed in writing at T-20), but that exemption walks you into two other regimes: (a) the Organized Camps law (HSC §30800 et seq. + Title 22 regs) with a permit from the county health department and a qualified on-site Health Supervisor; and (b) AB 506, which requires background checks, mandated-reporter training, and a child-abuse-prevention policy from youth-service organizations, we apply it in full regardless of the for-profit edge case. This plan complies with all three. human seam: county inspector

  • Thimble API (thimble.com/api), binds base General Liability by API, by the job, month, or year, and issues the certificate of insurance naming the venue host as additional insured, which is what actually unblocks the venue booking. verified API
  • Honest seam: the abuse & molestation endorsement, which county inspectors and sane parents require, is underwritten by specialists (camp insurers like K&K), not purchasable via any self-serve API. One broker call. ~$1,200/yr.
  • Checkr API, invite + run 1 adult background check (the instructor) at ~$65 incl. county fees. verified API
  • AB 506 / mandated-reporter training, online, ~$20 for the instructor, certificate auto-filed to the compliance folder.
  • County organized-camp permit application submitted online; county does one walk-through inspection at the venue (Phase 6). Fee ~$400.
Kill gate 1, No insurance binder + permit application accepted by T-16 → stop before the venue deposit moves (nothing is refundable-proof at that point). The county can simply be slow; that's a stop, not a workaround.
Phase 2 · T-20 → T-14 weeks (Feb–Mar)

Venue

~4 weeks (overlaps P1) · deposit $300
  • Requirements spec, written once and reused: 1,500+ sq ft makerspace, quiet interview room, secure outdoor access, walkable block, gallery wall, room screen or projector, on-site laser printer, reliable wi-fi sized for 8+ laptops with verifiable content filtering, single-use restroom adjacent to the main room (door visible from the instructor's seat), lockable on-site storage for the day-boxes and equipment June 12–25, weekday 8am–6pm access June 7–25 plus one Saturday 4-hour kitting block (June 12, 8am–12pm) plus one 30-minute Wednesday-evening restock block after Day 3 (June 16) plus one 3-hour Friday-afternoon print-tending block after Day 5 (June 18, the fixed-revision print window), COI naming the host.
  • Peerspace / Tagvenue, flagged honestly: neither exposes a public booking or inquiry API (verified Aug 2026; they are message-and-book marketplaces). no public API
  • Workaround: scripted marketplace search + browser automation builds a shortlist of 6 candidate venues with pricing and availability into Airtable; the operator makes one decision, pick + approve deposit (card on file). Hosts reply in-marketplace; the final booking is deliberately not scripted.
  • Budget anchor: ~$2,575/session (about 34.3 hours of room time at the ~$75/h makerspace rate: the ~25.8 session hours (8:45 prep through 11:15 pickup daily, 11:45 dismissal plus ~20-min gallery strike on Day 10), the 4-hour Session Zero kitting Saturday, plus the 30-minute mid-week restock after Day 3 (June 16), plus the 3-hour Friday-afternoon print-tending block after Day 5 (June 18, the fixed-revision print window), plus the ~1-hour equipment move-out after the Day 10 gallery strike (June 25)), deposit due at booking.
  • Venue agreement carries the break-glass cancellation-notice clause: the DocuSign venue agreement (signed by T-14 under kill gate 2) includes a clause authorizing the camp's designated break-glass adult, as named on the camp's keyholder roster at the time of notice, to send written notice cancelling or releasing the remaining dates on the camp's behalf; the notice must quote the clause and the roster entry, so the authority is on the contract, not on the keyholder's word alone. The clause also pins the notice's timing: the release notice is effective upon sending by the agreement's named notice method, not upon the venue's receipt, so on the night the clause matters, when the legal-notice address reaches the venue's legal entity but no person answers, the release is timed by the keyholder's sent notice, and the venue cannot later claim the cancellation was late or that remaining-date fees are owed. The clause also pins the break-glass notice's email sufficiency: at T-14 signing the founder reads the agreement's notice provision and the clause adopts its email path when one is named; if the venue's standard form names only certified mail, the break-glass clause adds email as a permitted method for this notice type only, so the keyholder at 9 PM sends the release by email from a phone and the effective-upon-sending timing attaches to the sent email, not to a certified-mail drop no one can reach after hours. The clause also pins the break-glass notice's sender identity to the roster name, not the sending domain: the keyholder at 9 PM sends from a personal phone with a personal sending address, so the notice's identity is the quoted roster entry, not the email domain, and the venue verifies the sender by the roster name it already holds, the name put in writing at signing, at the T-6w build, and with every keyholder change, so a personal sending address cannot dispute that the notice came from the camp's authorized sender. The clause also pins the break-glass notice's subject-line convention: the 9 PM email's subject line names the clause and quotes the remaining session dates, so the release is triage-visible in the venue's inbox on the night it goes out, not a clause-quoting body under a generic subject. The convention's dates are read off a printed page, never computed at 9 PM: the sealed break-glass envelope the T-6w build seals holds a one-page session calendar listing the ten teaching dates with their calendar dates, so the keyholder circles the dates printed after the broadcast night and pastes them into the subject line. The convention's clause name is never typed at 9 PM either: the sealed break-glass envelope holds a subject-line template beside the calendar, a printed page with the break-glass cancellation-notice clause's name already printed and a blank date line to fill, so the keyholder copies the circled dates onto the template's date line and reads the clause name off the template verbatim, typing no fixed text on the worst evening of the session, and a mistyped clause name can never undercut the triage visibility the convention exists for. The notice's quoted body is never typed at 9 PM either: the sealed break-glass envelope holds a printed clause-quote card and a printed roster card beside the calendar and the template, the clause-quote card carrying the break-glass cancellation-notice clause's full text printed from the signed agreement, with the clause's operative authority passage, the sentence granting the designated break-glass adult authority to send written notice cancelling or releasing the remaining dates, re-printed in an isolated box at the top of the card, the passage alone in larger type, the box assembled at print time by copying the passage verbatim from the card's own full-text print, never re-typed from memory, and read back against the signed agreement before the envelope is sealed, and the roster card carrying the keyholder's roster entry as the venue holds it, so the keyholder copies the boxed operative passage out of the isolated box and the roster entry into the email body verbatim, never starting from an adjacent unmarked line of the full text, and the authority the venue reads is the contract's own words, not the keyholder's memory of them, not the keyholder's choice of fragment, and not a box the founder re-typed. The roster card is re-printed in the re-walk sitting whenever the keyholder changes, so a 9 PM body can never quote a stale name; the clause-quote card is not re-printed on a keyholder change, because the clause did not change, so the re-sealed envelope holds the new roster card beside the untouched boxed card; a date shift moves neither the clause nor the roster, so the cards ride the date-shift re-print with their contents and their box unchanged, the date-shift re-seal printing a fresh envelope ID across the new band and retiring the old one, so the Day 1 photo read always covers the current envelope. If the session dates ever shift after signing, the founder re-prints and re-seals the envelope the same day the dates move, so no 9 PM trigger can fire on a stale calendar, and the template rides the re-print beside the calendar. The clause authorizes by role, not by name: the agreement is signed eight weeks before the T-6w build names the keyholder, so the roster always carries the current keyholder's name, and the venue's designated contact, the agreement's signing counterparty, and the agreement's legal-notice address are recorded on the roster at signing, the counterparty as the standing escalation fallback if the contact goes silent and the legal-notice address as the third line that cannot turn over with staff, read off the agreement's own notice provision at signing; the contact line is re-confirmed at the T-2w step the Phase 6 week-before window names; if no keyholder is named at signing, the founder sends the venue the roster name the week the keyholder is named, no later than the T-6w build, and whenever the keyholder changes afterward the founder sends the venue the updated roster name in writing as part of the re-walk the T-6w row requires, so the name the venue holds never lags the person who might send the notice.
Kill gate 2, No signed venue by T-14 → no June session. The venue is the long pole; there is no second choice without moving the cohort's dates.
Phase 3 · T-16 → T-10 weeks (Feb–early Apr)

Staffing: one instructor

~6 weeks (overlaps P2) · ~$300 sourcing + $2,000/session comp
  • AB5 settled, not dodged: the instructor is either the founder operating the camp (no classification question at all) or a W-2 employee on Gusto payroll (~$40/mo + ~$300 employer-side taxes per session), with California workers' comp bound before Day 1. Under the ABC test (AB5, Labor Code §2775) an on-site, on-schedule instructor delivering the camp's core service is an employee, so contractor arrangements, and the Deel contractor flow the original plan named, are off the table by design. Verify with employment counsel before launch.
  • Sourcing: one hire through local maker-space and school job boards (~$300 in listings). Upwork is flagged honestly, its hiring API is restricted (enterprise/managed access, not open for automated posting), so it is a backup, not the plan. restricted API
  • Screening: async video interview transcribed by a speech-to-text API and scored against a published rubric (kid-safety judgment ranked above technical skill); two reference calls scheduled by Twilio; decision recorded in Airtable.
  • Adjudication seam: a flagged background check goes to a human decision (FCRA, the Fair Credit Reporting Act, adverse-action review). No auto-reject, ever.
  • Structure: 1 instructor behind the 1:12 cap. The adult-in-the-room floor is the irreducible human core of this plan, see Honest Seams below. There is no degraded staffing mode: the format is built so one adult is sufficient, and anything that needs a second adult does not ship.
Kill gate 3, No instructor with a clean background check by T-10 → postpone. There is exactly one adult in the room; there is no smaller crew to fall back to.
Phase 4 · T-18 → T-4 weeks (Feb–mid May)

Camper acquisition

~14 weeks · ~$2,800
  • Meta Marketing API + Google Ads API, both real, both scriptable: launch, budget, pause, and report by code. verified APIs $2,500 spend plan; target CPL (cost per lead) $40–70, implied CAC (customer acquisition cost) ≈ $210/camper.
  • Funnel math (honest version): ~$2.5k spend → ~45 qualified leads → 12 enrollments at ~27% lead→paid conversion. If CPL runs > $90 for two straight weeks, ad sets pause automatically, the API kills its own spend.
  • Stripe Payment Links (created via API), $425 tuition, deposit option, scholarship codes. verified API Processing 2.9% + 30¢ ≈ $152/session.
  • Typeform API intake (camper info, allergies, pickup authorizations, accommodation requests) webhooked into Airtable; DocuSign API sends the liability waiver + photo release automatically on payment. verified APIs
  • Landing page: this site, enrollment CTAs activate when the live Payment Link publishes at T-18. Hosting is free (GitHub Pages); forms and payments are all API-side.
Kill gate 4 (soft, T-8), fewer than 6 paid → cut ad spend to $800 retarget-only. Hard, T-4: fewer than 10 paid → cancel session, refund in full via Stripe, run the wind-down checklist (the seven-step version the instructor-sick-day bullet enumerates: the T-6w build already scheduled all ten crisis broadcasts, so step one is disarming them and step six edits the greeting's TTS text). Binding re-score gate: if the post-pilot panel re-score has not published on this page by Friday, May 14, 2027, or clears below 9.0, the session cancels on the same terms, enrollments or not: 9.0 or the camp does not run. Cancelling at T-4 strands ≈ $4.4k (sunk ad spend + the pilot spend + non-refundable setup), that number is the plan's true worst case, and it's why the gates exist. Why ten: ten paid seats plus the two donor-funded scholarship seats equal the full 12-seat cohort, 12 x $425 = $5,100 against the $6,701 per-session operating budget (itemized in §07). A full session already runs $1,601 in the red, which the founding sponsorships absorb by design; at nine paid seats plus the two donor-funded scholarship seats, eleven revenue-generating seats, the hole is about $2,026 (11 x $425 = $4,675 against $6,701), and that gap would come out of the equipment-refresh reserve, so it cancels at the gate instead of burning the reserve.
Phase 5 · T-12 → T-2 weeks (Mar–early Jun)

Ops & equipment

~10 weeks (overlaps P3–P4) · equipment on the §07 startup list ($13,180)
  • Airtable API, the camper database of record: roster, med/allergy flags, pickup PINs + authorized-adult photos, incident log, daily attendance. verified API
  • Twilio, parent comms: enrollment confirmation, week-of logistics pack, daily 9:15 drop-off ping and 11:15 pickup ping (Day 10: 11:45, after the parents' showcase), emergency broadcast. ~$140/session. verified API
  • Equipment ordering, flagged honestly: Amazon Business has no public ordering API (punch-out procurement only). no public API Electronics (micro:bits, sensors, solder stock) order cleanly via Digi-Key / Mouser APIs (real order APIs); big-ticket items, the $13,180 startup list in §07 (2 printers, 3 rover kits, 8 refurbished laptops), are assembled as carts by the agent with one human checkout click. Order by T-8 so printers are commissioned and staff-trained by T-2.
Kill gate 5, Printers + laptop cart not commissioned by T-2 → switch Days 5/7 to a pre-negotiated partner makerspace (+$900 from contingency) rather than run untested tools with kids.

  • The week-before window holds one more digital task, about 30 minutes: pick the 12 blunders and lay out the blunder sheet. The Session Zero Hour 1 print run prints 36 blunder sheets (3 binders x 12, "this spring's blunders"), and the whole Day 1 AI-Fail thread runs through them: the lesson's 0:00-0:10 AI Fail of the Day opener dissects one, the 0:25-0:40 autopsy has each camper pick three and add each to the journal with one autopsy line, and the binders live in the Day 1 box, refreshed each year, but no step anywhere picks the 12 blunders, so the kitting Saturday would have printed 36 sheets off a selection that was never made. Pick the 12 in the week-before window. Selection rules: every blunder must be on the public record (news article, company postmortem, court record) with a cited source; age-appropriate for 10 to 14 (no graphic, sexual, or disturbing imagery, and no blunder whose punchline is someone getting hurt); spread across the failure types the camp teaches (hallucinated facts, bad advice taken literally, overconfident vision or captioning, wrong math, biased or unfair output); prefer blunders with a public correction or consequence, because the lesson is accountability, not mockery. Two of the 12 are standing picks, because they are the reason this camp exists: the 2023 case of the lawyer sanctioned after ChatGPT invented court citations, and Google AI Overviews' 2024 "glue on pizza" answer. The other ten are that spring's fresh picks, found with one news search per failure type. Lay out one sheet per blunder on the instructor laptop, one page each: the headline line; the what-happened line, one sentence; the where-it-was-reported line, publication and date, so the instructor can cite it at the opener; the why-it-failed line, naming the failure type; the takeaway line, what a producer checks before trusting this kind of output. The April pilot runs the Day 1 equivalent, so the pilot's prep week runs this same selection task once, picking that spring's 12, and the pilot's April copy-shop run prints the 24 sheets (12 pages x 2 binders) from the pilot-prep-week selection file. The June week-before window then refreshes any blunder that went stale between April and June and saves the pilot-prep-week selection file print-ready; the Session Zero Hour 1 run prints the 36 sheets from that file (12 pages x 3 binders), and Hour 2 slides 12 into each binder. The April pilot's prep week picks the 12 once and the June session reuses the saved file, the same way it reuses the Gauntlet deck; next spring the pilot-week picks a new 12, which is what "refreshed each year" means. the USB route's store scope is one store per day: the founder attempts the USB route only at the kiosk of the store where the hand-carry took its named exit, so before the hand-carry can close on a locked port the founder runs the two-part locked-port check: first a look for a physical cover panel or screw cap on the kiosk's USB port, distinguishing a covered port from an unfamiliar but open one, then one clerk question asking whether the kiosk's USB port takes customer print jobs, asked only where a clerk is reachable at an attended counter; a cover panel present confirms the port locked by definition with no clerk question needed, so the hand-carry closes on the logged failure as the recorded failure with the incident log as the evidence of record; a coverless port with no clerk reachable, a self-serve kiosk bank with no attended counter or a late-night or understaffed shift, also closes the hand-carry on the logged failure, because an unattended kiosk is not a guess the founder may make with the FAT32 stick; the logged no-clerk failure is terminal for the day at that store, so the founder may not re-enter the same store later that day to re-run the check once staff appear, because the logged failure already closed the hand-carry and a return would reopen it; a same-store re-entry takes the same closure, the hand-carry staying closed on the recorded failure with the incident log as the evidence of record, no new check and no new question; The terminality is for that calendar day only: the next calendar day, the founder may re-attempt the same store's kiosk once, running the full two-part check fresh as that day's one visit under the same one-store bound, because a clerk staffing the kiosk on a new day is a new condition, not a reopening of the closed hand-carry; the one re-attempt is the whole window, no same-store re-entry that next day and no second store's kiosk that day; and if the next-day attempt also closes on a locked-port failure, the founder marks the USB route unavailable on the card for the rest of the session and the card's email route becomes the pinned transfer path, so no third-day kiosk attempt happens; where the card already carries the USB-route-unavailable mark, the window never opens, because the check reads the mark, skips the USB attempt, and defaults to the card's pinned email-to-print address without a visit. A missed window has a bounded meaning: if the store's weekday hours on the card or the camp schedule keep the founder from reaching the store the next calendar day, the window rolls one day to the next day the founder can reach the store, still one visit, still the full two-part check fresh, still under the same one-store bound, and the founder logs the rolled window with time and the founder's initials; before the rolled visit leaves, the founder re-confirms the third store's weekday hours against the card, calling the card's phone number or checking the store's posted hours, because the card's hours were inked at the T-6w build and the store may have changed them since; on the call the founder also asks whether the store has any exception closure on the rolled day's date, a holiday shutdown, seasonal closure, inventory day, or other posted one-off the weekly hours never show; when the founder reads posted hours instead of calling, the founder reads the posted notice for exception-day postings too; the founder updates the card's weekday-hours line against a changed answer, logs the re-confirm with time and the founder's initials, the exception answer riding the same log line, and the roll's reachability reads the re-confirmed hours plus the exception answer, never the stale ink, so a store closed on the rolled day never reads as reachable: if the re-confirmed hours close the rolled day, or the exception answer covers the rolled day's date, the visit moves to the next day the re-confirmed hours, the exception answer, and the camp schedule allow, still one visit, still the full two-part check fresh, still inside the two-calendar-day end the roll already pins; if the store does not answer and posts no hours, the founder keeps the card's existing hours as the pinned fallback, logs the failed re-confirm attempt with time and the founder's initials, and the roll reads the existing hours, so a silent phone never strands the window with no read at all; the re-confirm is true at the call: a same-day emergency closure, an early close or a staff-event shutdown enacted after the call, could lock the door between the re-confirm and the founder's arrival. The founder pins the arrival-door read: at the door, before entering, the founder reads the door for any posted closure notice covering that day; if a posted notice covers the day, the founder logs the arrival-door read with time and the founder's initials (the notice's wording), does not enter, and the visit is not spent; if the posted notice is a partial-day notice, a sign reading 'closed until 2 PM' or 'opens late' that names a specific opening time that day rather than a full-day closure, the founder logs the arrival-door read with time and the founder's initials (the notice's wording and the stated opening time), does not run the bounded wait, because the notice already explains the dark door, and compares the stated opening time to the founder's visit window that day. The founder's visit window is the 90 minutes from 11:15 to 12:45 on the trip day, 11:45 to 1:15 when the trip day is Day 10; on a teaching day when the late-pickup escalation pushes dismissal past 11:15, the window starts at dismissal and still ends at 12:45, because the founder never leaves a waiting camper and the fixed end is what the stated-opening comparison reads against; the window must contain the full check, not just the door read: the check's own clock is fifteen minutes on the founder's phone clock, from the door read through the locked-port look, one clerk question, the send, and the ninety-second settle; the window never overlaps the 9:15-11:15 teaching block, because one adult cannot leave the room with campers in it; and the founder's window is not the shop owner's storefront visit window the Day 3 prep checklist re-confirms by text. If the founder can return to the store at or after the stated opening with the check's own fifteen minutes still inside that day's visit window, the visit is not spent, the founder returns once that same day at or after the stated opening, reads the door fresh, and runs the check if the door opens, a posted full-day notice or a new notice covering the rest of the day firing the arrival-door read normally with the visit not spent and the window rolling one day inside the two-calendar-day end, while a still-dark door with no new notice at the stated opening runs the bounded wait before the read fires, the two logged pulls standing as the waits, because a stale notice carries the exception answer's same freshness gap; if the door opens after the wait, the founder runs the check only with the check's own fifteen minutes still inside the window, otherwise logging the read without running the check, the visit not spent, the day treated as covered, and the window rolling one day inside the two-calendar-day end; if the stated opening falls after the founder's visit window that day, or fewer than fifteen minutes of the window remain at the stated opening, the founder treats the day as covered like a full-day closure, the visit not spent, the window rolling one day inside the two-calendar-day end, because an hours-long wait on the storefront is the same unstaffable loitering the bounded wait was built to end, and a check tail past the fixed end reopens the same unbounded errand; if the stated opening is already past when the founder reads the notice and the door is still dark, the founder treats the notice as stale and runs the bounded wait on the dark door, because staff running late is the most plausible explanation for a notice whose opening should already have come, and a stale notice offers no future opening for a return to read against; the two pulls stand as the waits, the first on arrival, the five minutes on the phone clock, each pull logged with time and initials, and a third pull is never taken; if the door opens during the wait the founder enters and runs the check only with the check's own fifteen minutes still inside the window, otherwise the founder logs the arrival-door read with time and the founder's initials (the notice's wording marked stale, plus the locked-door fact), does not enter, does not run the check, and the visit is not spent: the window rolls one day to the next day the re-confirmed hours, the exception answer, and the camp schedule allow, still one visit, still the full two-part check fresh, still inside the two-calendar-day end; a stale notice on an open door sends the founder in normally, the check running under the same fifteen-minute containment; Before any clocked branch runs, the founder checks the phone at the arrival-door read: if the phone is dead or its screen unreadable, the founder runs no clocked step at all, no stated-opening comparison, no bounded wait, and no check, because every trip-day clock, the window comparison, the bounded wait's five minutes, and the check's own fifteen minutes, reads the founder's phone clock, and a founder who guesses times can run a fifteen-minute check against a window that already closed with the incident log unable to verify a time it never read. The founder reads the posted notice, if any, and the door, and logs the arrival-door read with the founder's initials and the line 'phone dead, time unlogged' in place of the time, the notice wording recorded verbatim and the locked or dark door fact recorded beside it; the founder does not enter and does not run the check, and the visit is not spent: the window rolls one day to the next day the re-confirmed hours, the exception answer, and the camp schedule allow, and the return runs the full visit with a charged phone, the window comparison and the fifteen-minute check containment still governing. A dead or unreadable phone is the only branch that writes a log line without a time; every other branch still writes time and initials. The time-unlogged line sequences by visit, not by clock: the founder writes it in the day's trip log in the order the visits happened that day, and at the return the founder reconciles the hand-carry's evidence visit by visit, so the un-timed entry reads in its visit position between the timed entries around it, and an audit never time-sorts the hand-carry record, because a time sort would drop the un-timed line to the top or the bottom and read the visit out of order. The time-unlogged line stays time-unlogged through reconciliation: the founder never annotates it at the return with a recovered or estimated time, from the charged phone's step log, a drive receipt, or memory, because a backfilled estimate would read as a logged fact in an audit while the hand-carry record never read that time; the line's visit-order position is the audit's time reference, and charging the phone back up at the return does not rewrite the trip log. The dead-clock line's row in the timestamped incident log carries the row-timestamp marker: when the founder writes the arrival-door read into the log, the row's time field reads 'time unlogged, row timestamp is log write time, not read time', because the log auto-stamps every row with the write moment and an unmarked auto-timestamp on a time-unlogged line would be read as the arrival read time, the exact clock reading the no-backfill rule was built to keep out. The marker rides the row into every export and print, so no audit and no county inspector reads the write moment as the visit moment. On the paper backstop the marker takes the paper phrasing: when the founder writes the arrival-door read onto the one-page paper med/incident log sheet during an outage, the row's time field reads 'time unlogged, paper row has no auto timestamp, write time is not read time', because the paper sheet has no auto-stamp and a phrasing that names one would read as nonsense on the sheet; and at the evening transcription the founder writes the full marker into the Airtable row's time field, so the paper-to-Airtable path lands on the same marker the direct write carries and the transcription never drops the marker. Reconciled with §07 (above): this ledger replaces the sketch's $1,500 "marketing/licensing" line and its $600 operating insurance line, the two budgets don't double-count. All-in year-1 cash need: $12.1k launch + $13.2k equipment + $6.7k operating − $5.1k tuition ≈ $26.9k net, funded by sponsors/founder capital (the §07 numbers describe steady-state sessions, including the sponsor-underwritten $1,601/session shortfall; this is the year-1 cash picture). Workers' comp is $0 if the founder operates the camp themselves. Worst case (cancel at T-4): ~$4.4k stranded (the pilot spend is inside this by then). Prices are Aug-2026 estimates; ⚠-flagged items carry the most variance.

Honest seams, the humans this plan refuses to remove

Each seam is compressed to a single human decision, made once (or once a day), with everything else prepared by the stack:

  • 1 · County health inspector, the organized-camp permit requires a human walk-through and sign-off at the venue. One meeting, T-2 weeks.
  • 2 · Insurance broker, the abuse & molestation endorsement isn't self-serve on any API. One call, T-20 weeks.
  • 3 · Background-check adjudication, FCRA adverse-action review of any flagged candidate. A human reads, a human decides, per candidate.
  • 4 · Venue deposit approval, one decision on a shortlist of six. Money moves only after the click.
  • 5 · The adult in the room, one trained, background-checked, first-aid-certified adult on site every single minute campers are present, backed by the automated admin stack (Airtable, Twilio, Typeform). The 1:12 cap is a hard ceiling, and the 60-minute lesson format with self-directed project blocks is what makes one adult sufficient. This isn't overhead; it's the product.
  • 6 · Medication & incident command, the instructor administers meds and owns every incident call. Never automatable, never delegated to software.
  • 7 · Pickup authorization, photo-ID match against the signed authorization record, at the door, every day. The robot never dismisses a child.

API reality check (audited Aug 2026)

Every integration named in this plan, verified, including the ones that don't exist, because planning around a hallucinated API is how autonomous launches die:

ServiceStatusRole in the launch

Thimbleverified APIGeneral-liability bind (thimble.com/api), base GL only; rider via broker

Gustoverified APIW-2 payroll for the single instructor (contractor arrangements off the table; AB5 settled)

Digi-Key / Mouserverified APIsProgrammatic electronics ordering (sensors, micro:bits, solder stock)
Peerspace / Tagvenueno public APIShortlist via scripted search + browser automation; booking & deposit = human seam
Amazon Businessno public ordering APIAgent-assembled carts; one human checkout click for big-ticket equipment
Upwork hiringrestricted APIAgent-assisted posting via the UI; sourcing diversified to local boards

Correction for the record: an earlier draft cited "AB-2621" as the CA camp-licensing bill. Verified against leginfo, AB 2621 is not camp legislation (it's regional climate-planning in 2020, unemployment-insurance in 2022). The real anchors are HSC §1596.792 (child-care exemption), HSC §30800 et seq. + Title 22 (organized camps), and AB 506 (youth-org background checks + mandated-reporter training). This page keeps the receipts.

11 · The red team

We grade ourselves harder than any parent will.

This camp teaches kids to check AI's work. The least we can do is check our own. Before a single parent sees this plan, a seven-person panel red-teams it against one question: could one instructor plausibly teach this camp with one hour of direct instruction a day? The panel's scores and its demands are published here, including the ones we have not cleared yet. Re-scored 2026-09-07 after the device-free take-home option and the pilot evidence plan were published: 8.3/10. The plan ships no session until the panel clears 9.0.

The instructor · 8.5/10

"Teachable. The handouts exist."

The run-of-show plans, the 30-minute prep lists, and now the ten printed handouts are real lesson plans. The project block states plainly that the instructor stays in the room, silent, supervising. The remaining points are a pilot session: no paper plan, however complete, finds every surprise a real ten-year-old will invent.

The parent · 8.5/10

"The take-home question is answered."

The day is pinned to clock times (9:15 drop-off, 11:15 pickup), the project block's supervision is stated in plain language, and every project block runs on printed sheets with no device assumed. The device-free take-home option is now stated outright: paper paths for everything, no shared device goes home, conversation cards for the dinner table. Still open: the first real parent walkthrough night of the safety plan, scheduled for the week of the April 2027 pilot.

The 11-year-old camper · 8.5/10

"The days sound fun. Don't leave me stuck."

The handouts give the quiet 45 minutes a real structure: numbered steps, minute marks, a named job for finishing early, and a stuck protocol. A ten-year-old with no adult teaching can follow a printed page. The remaining points belong to a real ten-year-old in the room; paper cannot spend them.

The 14-year-old camper · 8/10

"Not babyish. Keep the hard versions real."

The senior extensions now sit inside the handouts themselves, not just the lesson plans: computing forecast error, writing the "what we almost let through" case study, sketching the rover waypoint plan. The core pace still skews young on paper; a pilot will tell whether the hard versions are hard enough. Flagged and fixed since the re-score: the pilot recruited ages 10 to 12 only, so the senior extensions had no testers and this card's open question could not be answered on pilot day. The cohort is now four 10-12s and four 13-14s, and the pilot's exit interview asks the 13-14s directly whether the hard versions were hard enough. Scores untouched; the points still belong to a real pilot day.

The safety officer · 8/10

"One adult works, if no minute goes unwatched."

Both earlier demands are cleared: continuous instructor presence through the project block, and the instructor named as the on-site Health Supervisor for the county organized-camp permit. No new findings this round; the remaining points belong to a real county inspector's walk-through, which no website can score.
